After the devastating realization at the river's edge, Leo was physically and emotionally spent. The walk back from Point State Park was a blur. Olivia pushed his chair, her hands steady and reassuring, while Luna and Stella flanked them, creating a tight physical barrier between Leo and the busy downtown sidewalks. 

They didn't have far to go. Downtown Pittsburgh—the "Golden Triangle"—was incredibly compact. In less than fifteen minutes, they were back in the quiet, air-conditioned lobby of The Three Rivers Grand Hotel. 

This was where Stella’s meticulous, fiercely protective planning revealed its true brilliance. A standard hotel checkout was at 11:00 AM. If they had followed the rules, they would be sitting in the lobby right now with all their luggage, staring down another nine hours before their midnight train. 

But Stella had simply bought another night. She had explicitly noted to the front desk that she was paying for a full extra night just so they could have a private, accessible sanctuary to crash in until 10:30 PM. The front desk staff, charmed by her upfront honesty and perhaps impressed by her unwavering dedication to her family, had gladly extended their keys.

As soon as the hotel room door clicked shut behind them, the outside world ceased to exist. 

Stella closed the heavy blackout curtains, plunging the room into cool, soothing darkness. Luna quietly turned down the blankets on Leo and Olivia's queen bed. Without a word, Olivia helped Leo transfer out of his chair and onto the mattress. 

He curled up on his side, burying his face in the pillows. The sensory deprivation of the dark, quiet room was an immediate balm to his overloaded nervous system. Olivia climbed into the bed beside him, wrapping her arms around him and pulling him flush against her chest. She provided the heavy, consistent deep pressure he needed, anchoring him to the mattress. 

"I've got you," she whispered into the dark. "Just rest."

For the next six hours, the hotel room was a tomb of recovery. Stella and Luna ordered quiet room service, eating by the dim light of the bathroom door so as not to disturb Leo. They read books and spoke in hushed whispers. There were no expectations, no masks to maintain, no ticking clocks to watch. There was only the fortress, doing what it did best.

By 10:00 PM, Leo had recovered enough spoons to sit up and eat a few bites of cold toast. The crushing panic of the afternoon had ebbed into a dull, manageable ache. He was still heartbroken about the journey west, but the intense physical paralysis had passed. 

"Alright, team," Stella said softly, checking her watch. "It's time. The station is only a five-minute bus ride from here. Let's get packed up."

They moved with practiced efficiency. By 10:45 PM, they had checked out and were standing at the 3RTA bus stop outside the hotel. The city was quiet, the streets mostly empty under the glow of the streetlights. 

A short bus ride dropped them off at Pittsburgh Union Station. It was a beautiful, historic building, but completely deserted at this hour, echoing with the soft hum of the ticketing kiosks. They navigated their way to the platform, the cool midnight air a stark contrast to the muggy afternoon. 

At exactly 11:45 PM, the massive silver Superliner cars of the ARTS *Capitol Limited* rolled into the station. 

Booking accommodations on the train had been a calculated compromise by Stella. Real-world ARTS wheelchair-accessible sleeper bedrooms only held two people. There was absolutely no way Leo would ever accept being separated from the rest of the pack in a different room. Therefore, Stella had deliberately booked the "Family Bedroom" on the lower level. It spanned the entire width of the train car and had enough beds to sleep all four of them together, but it came with a massive drawback: it was not wheelchair accessible, and it did not have an en-suite bathroom. 

They had to rely entirely on brute strength and zero modesty. While Stella and Luna normally had to work together to lift Leo, Olivia was incredibly strong. She effortlessly deadlifted Leo out of his manual chair and carried him straight down the public hallway to the tiny communal combination toilet-and-shower room. 

Because the door opened into the public corridor, it had to be firmly locked shut. This meant Olivia, Stella, and Leo were all crammed together inside a space barely larger than a closet. It was a chaotic, physically demanding process. They all stripped down so Stella could wash Leo, carefully maneuvering around the tiny sink and toilet, before Stella and Olivia quickly showered themselves. 

It was cramped and far from glamorous, but it meant they all got to sleep in the same room. As the train pulled out of Pittsburgh, heading into the dark expanse of Ohio and Indiana, Leo lay in the lower bunk of the Family Bedroom. The rhythmic rocking of the train was instantly soothing. He was heading the wrong way, deeper into the Midwest, but as Olivia’s hand reached down from the bunk above him to brush against his arm, he closed his eyes and let the train carry him into the night.

## The Windy City

### Part 1: Good Morning, Chicago

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 08:45 America/Chicago

The gentle rocking of the *Capitol Limited* came to a gradual halt. Leo blinked his eyes open in the lower bunk of their Family Bedroom. The night had been a blur of dark, rolling plains and the steady, rhythmic clatter of the tracks. 

"Good morning, Chicago!" Luna declared brightly, pulling back the heavy curtain on the cabin window. 

Sunlight flooded the tiny room, revealing a sweeping view of a massive railyard transitioning into a dense, towering skyline of glass and steel. They had officially arrived in the Midwest. 

The train pulled slowly into the grand, subterranean depths of Chicago Union Station. Unlike the smaller, quieter station in Pittsburgh, Chicago's terminal was a colossal, thrumming hive of activity. It was the great rail hub of America. 

After wrestling their bags out of the cramped cabin and successfully navigating Leo's chair down the narrow Superliner hallway and out onto the platform, they rode the elevator up to the Great Hall. The sheer scale of the room—with its towering Corinthian columns, sweeping marble staircases, and massive arched skylight—was breathtaking. 

"Alright," Stella said, surveying the bustling morning crowds of commuters. "We're officially on Central Time, so we gained an hour. It's almost 9:00 AM. Let's grab some breakfast right here in the station before we tackle the transit system."

They found a quiet corner in the food court, eating hot breakfast sandwiches and drinking strong coffee while watching the endless stream of people crisscrossing the Great Hall. Leo watched the crowds with a mix of awe and trepidation. The scale of the city was intimidating, but he was surrounded by his fortress, well-rested after the long night on the train. 

"So, where's the hotel?" Olivia asked, wiping a smear of ketchup from her thumb.

Stella tapped her clipboard. "I booked us into The Heritage House. It's an iconic, historic hotel right in the middle of The Loop, about a mile east of here. To get there, we get our first taste of the Chicago transit system."

"The CMA?" Luna asked, remembering the transit briefings Stella had given them weeks ago.

"Exactly," Stella nodded. "The Chicago Metropolitan Authority. We're going to buy our magnetic CityCards from the kiosks upstairs, and then we're going to catch a city bus straight down Jackson Boulevard. We drop the bags at the hotel, and then the city is ours."

Leo finished his sandwich and rested his hands on his wheels, looking up at the grand skylight of Union Station. They were deeper into the country now. The air felt different, the pace felt faster, and the architecture was staggering. The timer was still ticking, but the bright, bustling energy of Chicago was an incredible distraction. He was ready to explore.

### Part 2: Holding the Line

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 10:30 America/Chicago

The CMA bus ride down Jackson Boulevard was a sensory overload of towering skyscrapers and roaring city traffic, a stark contrast to the quiet, scenic arrival they’d had in Pittsburgh. By the time they reached the opulent, gilded lobby of The Heritage House, they were all thoroughly exhausted from the overnight train ride. 

Standard check-in wasn't until 3:00 PM, but Stella, anticipating the exhaustion of a 9:00 AM arrival, had called ahead weeks ago to guarantee an early check-in. The desk clerk handed over the keycards, and moments later, they were dropping their heavy bags on the floor of a sprawling, beautifully decorated room with two queen beds. 

The moment the door clicked shut, the adrenaline of navigating the city faded. The exhaustion of sleeping upright or in cramped train bunks caught up with all of them. 

Leo was the first to seek out comfort. His nervous system was humming with the low-level anxiety of the new city, and he desperately needed grounding. He transferred out of his chair and crawled onto the center of the nearest bed, looking up at Olivia with a silent, pleading expression. 

She understood immediately. Olivia kicked off her shoes and climbed onto the mattress beside him, wrapping her arms tightly around his torso and burying her face in the crook of his neck. The familiar pressure of her embrace was an instant anchor. 

But as Leo lay there, he noticed Luna standing near the window, her shoulders slumped, rubbing her arms. The overnight train had been hard on her, too. He reached out a hand, offering a silent invitation. Luna didn't hesitate. She crawled onto the bed on his other side, throwing a leg over his and pressing her forehead against his shoulder. 

A moment later, he felt the mattress dip near his feet. Stella, usually the indefatigable commander of their journey, let out a long, weary sigh. She kicked off her sensible walking shoes and crawled up from the foot of the bed, settling her head against Luna's shoulder and resting her hand on Leo's ankle. 

It was a massive, tangled, four-way cuddle. A physical manifestation of the fortress. 

Lying there in the center of it, entirely surrounded by the deep pressure and warmth of his entire world, Leo felt an immense sense of safety. They were a single, breathing entity, anchored together against the chaos of the city outside. 

Yet, even wrapped in this absolute security, a quiet, persistent ache pulsed in his chest. The transition to Central Time had deeply unsettled him. It wasn't just the fatigue of losing an hour of sleep; it was the psychological reality of the timezone itself. The clocks had shifted. The earth had physically rotated them further away from the sun they were used to. It was undeniable, geographical proof of how far West they had come, and how much closer they were to Seattle. 

The timer was ticking louder than ever. The realization threatened to spike his heart rate, pushing him toward the edge of another panic. 

But then Luna shifted, sighing happily in her sleep, her weight pressing heavier against him. Olivia tightened her arms around his waist, her breathing slow and steady. Stella’s hand was a warm, grounding anchor on his leg. 

The panic crested, hovered, and then slowly began to recede. The physical reality of the timezone change was terrifying, but the physical reality of the pack surrounding him was stronger. Being held so tightly by both of his sisters and his best friend was enough to hold the line against the grief. For now.

### Part 3: The Zenith Tower

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 14:00 America/Chicago

After an hour of quiet, grounding sleep tangled together on the hotel bed, the pack finally began to stir. The heavy knot of anxiety in Leo's chest hadn't entirely vanished, but the deep pressure from his sisters and Olivia had successfully pushed it back to a manageable simmer. 

"Alright," Stella declared, stretching her arms above her head and slipping back into her "tour guide" persona. "Who wants to go to the top of the world?"

Their destination for the afternoon was the Zenith Tower. At 110 stories tall, the massive, black-steel monolith was an architectural marvel and the undisputed king of the Chicago skyline. For years, it had held the title of the tallest building in the world. 

Neither Luna nor Leo had ever been in a skyscraper before. Growing up in the flat, coastal sprawl of Virginia Beach, the tallest things they usually saw were beachfront hotels. The prospect of riding an elevator to the 103rd-floor Skydeck was genuinely thrilling. 

"I want to see if we can see across Lake Michigan," Luna said excitedly as Olivia pushed Leo's wheelchair along the busy sidewalks toward the tower. 

<aac>I want to see the trains from above,</aac> Leo typed on his communicator, a rare spark of unburdened enthusiasm lighting up his eyes. 

After buying their tickets and passing through the crowded lobby, they packed into the high-speed elevator. The doors slid shut, and the elevator shot upward with a smooth, ear-popping intensity. A digital display above the door rapidly counted off the floors—10, 30, 50, 80—until finally, with a soft *ding*, they arrived at the 103rd floor.

The elevator doors opened, revealing a sprawling, panoramic observation deck enclosed entirely in massive panes of glass. The view was staggering. The city of Chicago spread out below them in a vast, sprawling grid of concrete, steel, and water. 

"Wow," Stella breathed, stepping out of the elevator. 

Olivia wheeled Leo right up to the nearest floor-to-ceiling window. Luna practically bounced with excitement as she ran up to stand beside his chair. 

"Look at this!" Luna grinned, pressing her hands against the glass. 

Leo leaned forward in his chair, gripping the armrests, eager to look for the tiny, toy-like trains weaving through the city grid below. He tilted his head down and looked straight past his footrests, past the window ledge, and down to the street. 

The sidewalk was 1,353 feet straight down. 

The cars looked like microscopic specks of dust. The people were entirely invisible. 

In a fraction of a second, the excitement evaporated. A primal, evolutionary alarm bell shattered Leo's consciousness. His nervous system didn't just spike; it short-circuited. His brain, suddenly processing the horrifying reality that he was suspended in a glass box a quarter of a mile in the sky, screamed at him that he was falling. 

He gasped, a sharp, choked sound, and threw himself backward in his chair, his hands flying up to cover his eyes. 

At the exact same moment, Luna let out a high-pitched, strangled yelp. She physically recoiled from the glass as if it had burned her, stumbling backward until her back hit the interior wall of the observation deck. 

"Nope. Nope. Nope," Luna gasped, her eyes wide with sudden, absolute terror. She sank to the floor, pulling her knees to her chest and wrapping her arms tightly around them. 

Leo was paralyzed in his chair, his chest heaving, his eyes squeezed shut so tightly it hurt. The vertigo was dizzying. Neither of them had known they were terrified of heights, because neither of them had ever been high enough to test it. 

Olivia and Stella, who had been admiring the view across the lake, spun around in alarm. 

"Whoa, hey," Olivia said, instantly abandoning the view and dropping to her knees beside Leo's chair. She grabbed his hands, pulling them gently away from his face. "Leo, look at me. Look at me."

Leo opened his eyes, but his gaze was wild and unfocused. He aggressively pointed a trembling finger toward the interior wall, desperate to get away from the glass. 

Stella was already crouched beside Luna, rubbing her back. "Okay, okay. I've got you," Stella murmured, realizing instantly what had happened. It was a shared, identical twin phobia, triggered at the exact same second. 

<aac>Get me away from the window,</aac> Leo typed frantically on his communicator, hitting the keys so hard the swing-arm rattled. 

Olivia didn't hesitate. She unlocked the brakes on his chair and quickly pulled him backward, turning the chair around so he was facing the solid interior wall, right next to where Luna was curled up on the carpet. 

The Skydeck, which just a minute ago had been the thrilling highlight of the day, was now a 103-story prison.

### Part 4: The Descent

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 14:15 America/Chicago

The 103rd-floor Skydeck, a bustling tourist attraction full of chattering families and flashing cameras, felt like a vacuum of terror for the twins. 

Luna was crying now, quiet, gasping sobs as she stayed curled in a tight ball against the interior plaster wall. She had her eyes squeezed shut, refusing to look anywhere near the perimeter of the room. "I want to go down," she whimpered, her voice tight with panic. "Stella, please. I want to go downstairs. *Now*."

Stella, who had paid a small fortune for the four Skydeck tickets, didn't hesitate for a single microsecond. The view, the money, the experience—none of it mattered. "Okay," Stella said softly, her voice an anchor of absolute, unwavering calm. "We're leaving right now. Do not open your eyes, Luna. Just listen to my voice."

Leo was still trembling violently in his wheelchair, his chest heaving as his own brain struggled to process the adrenaline spike. He was parked directly beside Luna, facing the same blank interior wall. 

Olivia stood up, moving to grab the handles of Leo's wheelchair, but Luna reached out a shaking hand and grabbed the metal frame of the chair first. 

"I'll push him," Luna gasped, struggling to her feet while keeping her back pressed firmly against the wall. She didn't want Olivia to do it. She needed the physical anchor. She needed to feel the heavy, grounding weight of her twin's wheelchair under her hands to prove she was tethered to something solid. 

"You got it," Olivia said smoothly, stepping aside and moving to flank them instead, creating a physical barrier between the twins and the horrifying glass windows. 

With Luna gripping the push-handles like a lifeline and keeping her eyes locked firmly on the carpet, she pushed Leo blindly forward as Stella guided them straight back to the elevator banks in the center core of the building. 

The moment they were inside the elevator and the heavy steel doors slid shut, sealing them inside a windowless, solid box, the collective relief was palpable. 

The elevator began its rapid, ear-popping descent. 

Luna immediately let go of the push-handles and stepped around to the front of the wheelchair. She sank to her knees right on the elevator floor and threw her arms around Leo's neck, burying her tear-streaked face in his shoulder. 

Leo didn't have the motor control to hug her back effectively, but he leaned his head heavily against hers, pressing his cheek against her hair. He closed his eyes, focusing entirely on the solid pressure of her embrace and the familiar scent of her shampoo. 

Both of them were hyperventilating, their chests heaving in synchronized, ragged gasps as the digital display above the door rapidly counted down the floors. 100... 80... 50... 

Stella stood behind the wheelchair, placing one hand firmly on Luna's shoulder and the other on the back of Leo's neck. It was a silent, steadying pressure. Olivia stood close by, her presence a quiet guard over the pack. 

"Breathe, you two," Stella murmured gently over the hum of the descending elevator. "You're safe. We're in a solid box. We're going back to the ground."

"I hated that," Luna choked out, her grip on Leo tightening. "I hated that so much."

"ME TOO," Leo's communicator chimed, his thumb trembling as he hit the pre-programmed button. 

The digital display hit *1*, and the elevator slowed to a smooth stop. The doors slid open, revealing the bustling, windowless, solid-stone lobby of the Zenith Tower. 

They had made it back to ground level. The terrifying ordeal was over, but it had drained whatever energy the afternoon nap had given them. The twins were exhausted, their nervous systems completely fried. The Midwestern leg of the great journey had officially begun with a spectacular misfire.

### Part 5: Solid Ground

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 14:20 America/Chicago

The ground-floor lobby of the Zenith Tower was a cavernous, echoing space lined with heavy travertine marble and polished black granite. It was solid, windowless, and reassuringly attached to the earth. 

Stella guided them to a quiet alcove near the security desk, far away from the bustling queues of tourists waiting to ascend to the Skydeck. As soon as the wheelchair came to a stop, the adrenaline that had fueled their frantic escape finally crashed. 

Luna, who was still kneeling in front of Leo, buried her face in his shoulder again, her shoulders shaking with fresh, quiet sobs. The sheer, primal terror of the experience was finally catching up to her. 

Leo leaned his head against hers, tears spilling hot and fast down his own cheeks, fogging up his thick glasses. His nervous system was completely fried. He hadn't just been scared; he had been genuinely, fundamentally convinced that he was going to fall to his death. 

"I'm sorry," Luna choked out, her voice muffled against his t-shirt. "I'm so sorry, Leo. We didn't know. I didn't know."

<aac>It is okay,</aac> he managed to type, his thumb shaking so badly he hit the wrong key twice before getting the message out. 

It was a profound, startling realization for both of them. In eighteen years, neither twin had ever been higher than a third-floor balcony. They simply hadn't known they possessed a shared, debilitating fear of extreme heights. But it made a twisted kind of sense; their brains were wired with so many identical quirks and sensitivities, it was only natural that their phobias would match, too. 

As they clung to each other, crying and trying to regulate their ragged breathing, Leo felt a different kind of ache flare up in his chest. The panic of the tower was fading, but the heavy, relentless dread of the ticking clock was rushing in to fill the void. 

He lifted his head from Luna's shoulder and looked up, his tear-filled eyes desperately searching the alcove. 

Olivia was standing right there. She didn't need him to ask. She immediately knelt down beside Luna, wrapping her long arms around both of the twins in a tight, encompassing embrace. 

Leo slumped against her side, letting out a long, shuddering exhale. He needed the deep pressure of his twin sister to recover from the phobia, but he needed the specific, unwavering warmth of Olivia to remind him that she was still here. That she hadn't left for Washington state yet. That they were still together on solid ground. 

Stella stood a few feet back, giving them the space they needed while maintaining a watchful perimeter. She leaned against the cool marble wall, letting out a long, quiet breath of her own. Her heart was still pounding from the suddenness of the crisis. 

For several long minutes, the alcove was quiet except for the muffled sounds of the twins crying, anchored together in the safety of the fortress. The polished marble floor beneath them was hard, cold, and beautifully, undeniably solid.

### Part 6: Deep Dish

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 15:30 America/Chicago

After twenty minutes of quiet recovery in the Zenith Tower lobby, the twins' tear-streaked faces had dried, and the acute panic had receded into a dull, manageable exhaustion. 

"Okay," Stella said, pushing herself off the marble wall. "We need a reset. We need something heavy, warm, and distinctly ground-level."

A quick conversation with a sympathetic security guard pointed them toward Russo's, a hole-in-the-wall pizzeria a few blocks away. It wasn't one of the flashy, tourist-trap chains with neon signs; it was a dimly lit, brick-walled establishment tucked down a side street, smelling powerfully of garlic, oregano, and baking dough. 

They snagged a corner booth, parking Leo's wheelchair at the end of the table. The ambiance was cozy and wonderfully cavernous—the exact opposite of a glass box suspended in the sky. 

When the waitress brought their order out thirty minutes later, it didn't look like a pizza. It looked like a cast-iron pie pan filled to the brim with a literal mountain of bubbling cheese, chunky tomato sauce, and thick, savory sausage. 

"Oh my god," Luna breathed, her eyes widening as the waitress used a heavy steel spatula to lift the first slice. Thick ropes of mozzarella stretched and pulled, refusing to let go of the pan. 

Stella stared at the monstrous slice as it was deposited onto her plate. The crust was nearly two inches deep. "I am going to need a nap after just looking at this," she muttered, picking up her fork. 

Olivia cut Leo's slice into manageable, bite-sized pieces for him. He used his fork to spear a chunk of the thick, buttery crust covered in cheese and sauce. The moment it hit his tongue, his eyes closed in sheer bliss. It was incredible. It was heavy, rich, and incredibly comforting—exactly the kind of grounding sensory input his fried nervous system craved. 

<aac>This is so good,</aac> Leo typed, reaching eagerly for his second bite. 

"It really is," Luna agreed, her mouth full. The terrifying ordeal at the Zenith Tower seemed a million miles away, replaced by the warm, buzzing atmosphere of the restaurant and the unbelievable weight of the food. 

Stella managed exactly one slice before she had to lay her fork down in defeat, pressing a hand to her stomach. "It's amazing," she admitted, looking at the massive, half-eaten pizza still sitting in the center of the table. "But I think my stomach just waved a white flag. That is an obscene amount of cheese."

Olivia, possessing the legendary metabolism of a tall, athletic nineteen-year-old, happily reached for her third slice. 

Leo watched his best friend eat, a quiet, contented warmth settling in his chest. The heavy food, the dimly lit brick walls, and the lively chatter of the pack were successfully drowning out the ticking clock, at least for the moment. He took another bite of his own pizza, letting the rich flavors ground him firmly to the earth.

### Part 7: The Cheese Toll

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 16:45 America/Chicago

The walk back to The Heritage House from the pizzeria was significantly slower than their outbound journey. The adrenaline crash from the Zenith Tower, combined with the sheer, undeniable gravity of Chicago deep-dish pizza, had turned the pack into a slow-moving caravan.

But nobody was struggling quite as much as Stella. 

"I think I made a terrible mistake," Stella groaned, a hand pressed firmly against her stomach as they waited at a crosswalk. She looked slightly pale, her usual commanding posture replaced by a slight, pained hunch. 

"You only had one slice, Stel," Luna pointed out, though she was rubbing her own stomach sympathetically. 

"That wasn't a slice, Luna. That was a brick of solid dairy wrapped in dough," Stella muttered, wincing as a sharp cramp hit her abdomen. "My body is actively rejecting it. We need to get back to the room. Now."

Olivia chuckled softly, easily pushing Leo's wheelchair over the curb as the light changed. "The guardian bear has been defeated by cheese."

"Laugh it up," Stella wheezed, picking up her pace despite her obvious discomfort. "Just wait until you hit your mid-twenties. Your iron stomach will betray you too."

By the time they reached the opulent lobby of their hotel, Stella was speed-walking. The moment she unlocked their door, she tossed the keycard onto the nearest bed, bypassed her luggage entirely, and made a beeline straight for the bathroom. 

The door shut with a solid *click*, followed immediately by the sound of the locking mechanism sliding into place. 

"I claim this territory!" Stella called out from behind the door, her voice echoing slightly against the tile. "No one is allowed in here for the foreseeable future. Save yourselves!"

Luna snorted, collapsing back-first onto one of the queen beds. "I think she's dying."

<aac>She will be okay,</aac> Leo typed, a small, amused smile playing on his lips. It was rare to see their indestructible older sister so thoroughly sidelined by something as mundane as lunch. 

"Yeah, she'll survive," Olivia agreed, dropping her own bags near the desk and stretching her arms over her head. "But I think our sightseeing is officially done for the day."

"I'm fine with that," Luna sighed, closing her eyes. The heavy food had successfully anchored her frayed nerves, but the emotional exhaustion of the panic attack was finally demanding its due. "I just want to lie here and not think about falling from the sky."

With Stella completely occupying the bathroom and Luna sinking into a food coma on the bed, Olivia walked over to Leo's wheelchair. She knelt down in front of him, resting her hands lightly on his knees. 

"You doing okay?" she asked softly, her green eyes searching his face. "That was a pretty rough afternoon."

Leo nodded slowly. The phobia was gone, safely locked away behind the memory of solid ground and heavy food. But sitting here in the quiet hotel room, with the late afternoon sun slanting through the windows, the other, quieter ache returned. 

He reached out and gently laid his hand over hers. 

<aac>I know,</aac> his communicator said, though he hadn't typed it recently. It was a saved phrase he kept readily available. He knew she had to go. He knew the clock was ticking. 

Olivia's expression softened, a deep, empathetic sadness in her eyes. She squeezed his knee. "I know too, Leo," she whispered. 

She stood up, stepped around to the side of his chair, and carefully lifted him into her arms. But instead of carrying him to the empty queen bed, she carried him over to the bed where Luna was currently dozing. Olivia knew that while Leo desperately needed her warmth, he still needed the physical anchor of his twin sister to fully regulate his nervous system after the panic attack. 

Olivia gently laid him down on the mattress right beside Luna. Luna stirred, blindly reaching out to wrap an arm around her brother before sinking back into her food coma. Olivia kicked off her own shoes and climbed into the bed on his other side, wrapping her arms around his torso to complete the hold. Together, they formed a quiet, three-person knot of absolute security, listening to the distant, muffled groans of their oldest sister in the bathroom.

### Part 8: Recovery

**Date:** 2003-06-25 at 18:00 America/Chicago

The sun had just begun to dip below the Chicago skyline, casting long, golden shadows across the hotel room, when the bathroom door finally unlocked. 

Stella emerged looking thoroughly defeated. She was pale, her hair was slightly disheveled, and she was clutching her stomach as if it had personally betrayed her. 

"I am never eating cheese again," she announced weakly, shuffling across the carpet toward the empty queen bed. "Ever. For the rest of my natural life."

Luna lifted her head from the pillow, blinking sleepily. "You survived."

"Barely," Stella groaned, collapsing face-first onto the mattress. She didn't even bother pulling down the comforter. "I threw up. My stomach muscles are sore. That deep dish is an absolute weapon of mass destruction."

"I told you the guardian bear had fallen," Olivia teased gently from her spot beside Leo, though she kept her voice low. 

"The bear is in hibernation," Stella mumbled into the mattress. "Do not perceive me. Do not expect anything from me. I am not leaving this bed until tomorrow morning."

"Are you going to be okay?" Luna asked, sitting up slightly, her protective instincts overriding her exhaustion. 

Stella rolled onto her side with a heavy sigh, clutching a pillow to her abdomen. "I'll be fine, Luna. I just need to sleep it off. I'm usually fine with normal dairy—like cereal or a regular slice of pizza—but my body just aggressively reminded me that I have a strict lactose limit. And that pizza was way, way past it."

"We can order room service for dinner later," Olivia suggested, gently stroking Leo's messy hair. "We don't need to go anywhere else tonight. I think we've hit our quota for trauma and dairy."

"Room service sounds amazing," Stella whispered, her eyes already sliding shut. "Just... order me some plain toast. Or maybe some ice chips."

Within minutes, the soft, rhythmic sound of Stella's breathing filled the room, joining the quiet hum of the air conditioner. 

Leo lay between Luna and Olivia, the heavy, comforting weight of the blankets and his pack anchoring him to the bed. The day had been an absolute rollercoaster—from the sensory overload of arriving in a new timezone, to the paralyzing terror of the Skydeck, to the rich, grounding comfort of the deep dish pizza. 

He didn't want to think about the ticking timer counting down to Seattle. He didn't want to think about the timezone, or the miles between here and home, or how fragile their fortress was. 

Instead, he focused entirely on the immediate present. His stomach was pleasantly full. His older sister was safely resting just a few feet away. His twin was a warm, familiar weight on his left side, and his best friend was an unwavering presence on his right. For tonight, the world was exactly the size of this hotel room, and for tonight, that was enough.

## The Heartland

### Part 1: The BRAT Diet

**Date:** 2003-06-26 at 08:30 America/Chicago

The next morning, the heavy curtains of their room at The Heritage House were pulled back to reveal a bright, cloudless Chicago day. But inside the room, the pace was significantly slower than the morning before. 

Stella was sitting propped up against the headboard, looking a little better but still noticeably fragile. The color hadn't quite returned to her cheeks, and she was moving with a stiff, careful deliberation to avoid aggravating her sore abdominal muscles. Luna was sitting at the foot of the bed, keeping a watchful, protective eye on her older sister.

The heavy hotel door clicked open, and Olivia backed into the room, pulling Leo's wheelchair inside. She had understood implicitly that Leo didn't want to be separated from her for even a ten-minute errand, so they had gone down to the lobby together while Luna stayed upstairs. 

Olivia set a plastic bag from a nearby convenience store down on the desk and began pulling items out with the brisk, no-nonsense efficiency of a triage nurse. 

"Alright, Stella. Breakfast is served," Olivia announced. She handed Stella a bruised yellow banana, a small plastic cup of unsweetened applesauce, and two slices of dry, unbuttered toast they had snagged from the hotel’s continental breakfast downstairs. 

Stella looked at the grim spread, her nose wrinkling in distaste. "Olivia, I'm feeling better. I don't need to eat like a toddler."

"You threw up yesterday," Olivia countered, crossing her arms over her chest and raising a single, challenging eyebrow. "You pushed your body past its lactose breaking point. You are on the BRAT diet today. Bananas, Rice, Applesauce, and Toast. Non-negotiable."

"I am the adult here," Stella muttered, though she didn't make any move to put the banana down. 

"And I'm the one who usually carries Leo when things go sideways, which means my vote counts double," Olivia replied smoothly. "Eat the toast, Stel. It's for the best."

Luna, who was happily munching on a chocolate chip muffin on the other bed, giggled. "Just listen to Dr. Olivia, Stella. You're the one who always tells us to respect our biological limits."

Stella let out a long, defeated sigh and picked up the dry toast. "Fine. But I am filing a formal grievance against Chicago's culinary scene."

Leo smiled from his wheelchair, his communicator already swung into place. <aac>What are we doing today?</aac> he typed, hitting the play button. 

"Well," Stella said, taking a cautious, joyless bite of her toast. "We are definitely not going anywhere high up. And given that my stomach is still sore, we are not doing anything that requires running or a massive amount of walking. Luckily, The Heritage House is right in the middle of everything."

She pulled a city map from the nightstand. "Right across the street is Lakeside Park. It's flat, it's on the ground, and it's right on the edge of Lake Michigan. We can walk down to the Grand Fountain, and if we're feeling up to it, the Lakefront Aquarium is just south of there. It's fully wheelchair accessible, completely air-conditioned, and has lots of fish. Does that sound safe and reasonable to everyone?"

<aac>Fish are good,</aac> Leo typed immediately. The aquarium sounded perfectly safe. It was on the ground, it was indoors, and there were zero glass boxes suspended in the sky. 

"I'm in," Luna agreed, finishing her muffin. "But if you need to stop and sit down, Stella, you have to tell us."

"Deal," Stella said, looking mournfully at her applesauce. "As soon as I finish this incredibly depressing breakfast, we'll head out."

### Part 2: Fountains and Fishes

**Date:** 2003-06-26 at 10:00 America/Chicago

The morning sun reflected brilliantly off the surface of Lake Michigan as the pack slowly made their way through Lakeside Park. After the terrifying verticality of the Zenith Tower, the sprawling, manicured green space was exactly the kind of massive, horizontal grounding the twins needed. 

They started at the Grand Fountain, a colossal, ornate water feature that shot massive jets of water high into the air. Leo loved the thundering sound of the water crashing back into the tiered marble basins. It was chaotic but rhythmic, a predictable kind of sensory input that felt safe. 

"Okay, this is infinitely better than looking down from a glass box," Luna declared, sitting on the stone retaining wall and letting the fine, cool mist spray across her face. 

"Agreed," Stella said, sitting down heavily next to her. She was moving at half her usual speed. 

From the fountain, it was a short, paved walk south to the Lakefront Aquarium. The massive, classical stone building sat right on the edge of the water. For the next two hours, they wandered through the cool, dimly lit, air-conditioned halls. 

For Leo, the aquarium was a sanctuary. The slow, graceful movements of the stingrays, the mesmerizing glow of the jellyfish tanks, and the quiet, bubbling hum of the massive filtration systems were incredibly soothing. He sat parked in front of a massive, multi-story Caribbean reef tank, completely captivated by a sea turtle lazily swimming past the glass. 

Olivia stood right behind his chair, resting her hands on the push-handles. Every so often, she would lean down and point out a particularly colorful fish, her voice a low, comforting murmur in his ear. 

"I like the turtle," she whispered. "He looks like he's got nowhere to be."

<aac>He is very relaxed,</aac> Leo typed back, a small smile on his face. He leaned his head back against her arm, perfectly content. 

A few yards away, Stella was sitting on a padded bench in front of a penguin exhibit. She had her elbows resting on her knees, rubbing her temples. Luna was sitting next to her, watching her older sister with a hawk-like intensity. 

"You're done, aren't you?" Luna asked quietly. 

Stella let out a long, exhausted sigh. "I think the guardian bear needs to tap out. My stomach isn't actively hurting anymore, but my energy reserves are completely shot. I am running on fumes and applesauce."

She pulled up the sleeve of her jacket and checked her watch. It was 12:15 PM. 

"Alright," Stella announced, raising her voice slightly to get Olivia and Leo's attention. "We need to wrap this up. We have to grab our bags from the hotel bell desk and get to Union Station."

"When does our train leave?" Olivia asked, turning Leo's chair away from the reef tank. 

"The *Western Pioneer* departs exactly at 2:00 PM," Stella said, slowly pushing herself up from the bench. "And since I am moving at the speed of a wounded turtle today, we need to leave plenty of buffer time."

"Next stop: Denver," Luna said, a hint of excitement returning to her voice. 

The mention of the next city sent a familiar, dull ache through Leo's chest. Chicago had been chaotic and exhausting, but leaving it meant boarding another train. It meant moving further West. The clock was still ticking, and with every mile they covered on the *Western Pioneer*, Olivia’s departure in Seattle drew closer. 

But as Olivia pushed his wheelchair toward the exit, she squeezed his shoulder. He reached up and patted her hand. They were still together. The clock was ticking, but the alarm hadn't gone off yet.

### Part 3: Boarding the Pioneer

**Date:** 2003-06-26 at 13:30 America/Chicago

Chicago Union Station was a cavernous, echoing cathedral of transit. Even with the mid-day crowds, the sheer scale of the Great Hall made the pack feel small. 

They retrieved their bags from The Heritage House bell desk and made their way to the boarding platforms. Stella was moving slowly, her face pale, letting Olivia take the lead. The deep dish pizza incident had completely depleted her energy reserves, and she was currently operating on pure stubborn willpower. 

"Okay," Olivia said, turning to face the pack as they waited near the track for the *Western Pioneer*. She had naturally slipped into the commander role. "Stella, your only job is to get on the train and sleep. I've got the bags, I've got Leo, and I've got the elevation protocols."

"Elevation protocols?" Luna asked, tilting her head. 

"We are heading to Denver," Olivia explained, slipping effortlessly into "triage nurse" mode. "It's the Mile High City. We are currently sitting at sea level. By tomorrow morning, we'll be at five thousand, two hundred and eighty feet. That means less oxygen, which means we all need to be aggressively hydrating starting right now, and we need to pace ourselves when we get there."

Leo listened carefully. His cardiovascular system had never been tested at a high altitude before. The prospect of thin air was slightly intimidating, but hearing Olivia take charge of the medical logistics was incredibly reassuring. 

<aac>Hydrate,</aac> his communicator stated, and he obediently reached for the water bottle attached to the frame of his wheelchair. 

"Exactly," Olivia smiled, giving his shoulder an affectionate squeeze. "Drink up. The air on the train is dry anyway."

At 1:30 PM, the boarding call for the sleeper cars echoed through the station. 

Just like their journey from Pittsburgh, Stella had booked the Family Bedroom on the lower level of the sleeper car. The real-world logistics of rail travel meant it was the only room large enough to keep the four of them together, even though it wasn't officially wheelchair accessible and lacked a private bathroom. 

Once they navigated the narrow hallway, the brutal, cramped reality of the Family Bedroom became apparent. To get all four of them in one room, sacrifices had to be made. 

Stella, pale and clutching her stomach, immediately called dibs on the Adult Upper bunk. It was narrow, but it was six feet long, allowing her to stretch out her cramping abdomen. She climbed the small ladder, kicked off her shoes, and pulled a blanket over her head. "Goodnight, Chicago," her muffled voice came from above. "Wake me up when we hit the mountains."

"Get some rest, Stel," Olivia murmured, turning to the remaining beds. 

Luna looked at the remaining two beds on the opposite wall: a tiny upper and lower bunk meant for small children, both under five feet long. With a resigned sigh, Luna claimed the 4'9" Child Lower bunk, immediately drawing her knees to her chest and curling into a tight fetal position to fit. 

That left the Adult Lower bunk. At 40 inches wide, it was exactly the size of a standard twin bed. It was technically meant for one adult, but it was the only option left. 

Olivia effortlessly lifted Leo from his wheelchair and settled him into the Adult Lower bed, then squeezed in right beside him, her back pressed against the wall. She wrapped her long arms around him, pulling him tightly against her chest to keep him from rolling off the narrow mattress. 

Normally, Leo loved this kind of extreme closeness and deep pressure from any member of his pack. But right now, he specifically wanted Olivia. He didn't know if he would ever get to see her again after this summer. The ticking clock was a constant, terrifying drumbeat in his mind, and being held this tightly by her was the only way to silence it. 

Because Stella was out of commission in the top bunk, the physical burden of the journey had shifted entirely to Olivia's athletic shoulders. She would be the one carrying him down the hall to the tiny, cramped shower room tonight. She would be the one lifting him back into his chair tomorrow morning. 

It was a stark, unignorable preview of what was going to happen on the return trip. When they boarded the train in Seattle to head back East, Olivia wouldn't be with them. And if Stella or Luna were compromised, the entire caregiving infrastructure of their fortress would instantly collapse. 

A sharp spike of anxiety hit his chest, completely unrelated to the impending altitude change. He pushed the terrifying thought away, forcing himself to focus on the gentle hum of the train's massive diesel engines spinning up. 

At exactly 2:00 PM, a long, low horn blast echoed through the station. The *Western Pioneer* lurched forward, slowly rolling out of the dark station and into the bright Midwest afternoon. The great journey West had resumed.

### Part 4: Cramped Quarters

**Date:** 2003-06-26 at 20:00 America/Chicago

By 8:00 PM, the *Western Pioneer* was rolling steadily through the darkening cornfields of Iowa. Inside the Family Bedroom, the air was close and warm. Stella was still fast asleep in the Adult Upper bunk, having barely moved since they left Chicago. 

"Alright," Olivia said, sitting up carefully so she wouldn't bump her head on the low ceiling. "Bathroom and shower time. Let's get this logistical nightmare over with."

Luna enthusiastically unfolded herself from the Child Lower bunk, wincing as her joints popped. "Yes, please. If I stay in the fetal position any longer, I'm going to permanently fuse into a ball."

Because their cabin didn't have an en-suite, the evening routine required a public excursion down the narrow hallway of the lower level to the communal combination toilet-and-shower room. 

Olivia effortlessly deadlifted Leo out of the narrow bed and carried him out the cabin door. Luna followed closely behind, carrying the clean clothes and the shower bag. The rocking of the train made the walk down the corridor a wobbly, bouncing affair. 

When they reached the communal bathroom, the three of them crowded inside and locked the door behind them. The space was incredibly cramped—barely larger than a standard closet—with a toilet on one side and a tiny shower nozzle on the other. Because the door opened directly into the public hallway, there was no option to leave it propped open for space. They had to make it work with the door shut. 

Olivia carefully undressed Leo and lowered him onto the small, fold-down plastic shower seat. Once he was secure, the two girls began undressing to take their own showers and use the facilities, navigating the tiny space with the casual, practiced modesty of the pack. 

Luna grabbed the sponge and the body wash, stepping into the narrow spray of the shower to begin washing her brother. Olivia stood nearby, bracing herself against the fiberglass wall as the train swayed, waiting to help rinse him. 

"Make sure you get his back first, it's easier while he's sitting up straight," Olivia suggested casually. 

Luna paused, the sponge hovering over Leo's shoulder. She looked up at Olivia, her mouth opening as if she was about to correct her, to explain the exact, methodical routine that she and Stella had perfected over the years. She wanted to say: *No, you always start with his arms so you don't overstimulate his core too quickly.* She wanted to teach Olivia how to do it perfectly. 

But the words died in her throat. 

Leo watched his twin's face fall. He saw the sudden, sharp heartbreak in her eyes as the realization hit her. There was no point in teaching Olivia the routine. There was no point in passing on the intricate, specialized knowledge of how to bathe him, because in just a few days, Olivia was going to get off a train in Seattle and never come back. 

Luna swallowed hard, looking quickly down at the sponge so Olivia wouldn't see the tears welling in her eyes. "Right. The back," she whispered, her voice thick with unshed emotion. 

Leo felt a heavy, crushing ache in his own chest. He reached out a wet hand and gently grasped Luna's wrist. He couldn't speak, but he squeezed her wrist twice—their silent twin code for *I know. I feel it too.*

Luna gave him a watery, devastated smile, gently resting her forehead against his wet shoulder for just a second before she resumed washing him. 

Olivia, blissfully unaware of the silent heartbreak passing between the twins, hummed quietly over the rumble of the train tracks. They finished the cramped, chaotic showering process in silence, toweling off and getting dressed in the tiny space before Olivia lifted Leo once more to carry him back to their room.

### Part 5: Crossing the Plains

**Date:** 2003-06-26 at 21:00 America/Chicago

When Olivia carried Leo back into the Family Bedroom, the small cabin felt noticeably different. The heavy, oppressive silence of Stella's sleep had lifted. 

Stella was sitting up in the Adult Upper bunk, her legs dangling over the edge. She still looked tired, but the pallor had faded from her cheeks. 

"Look who's alive," Luna said, tossing the damp shower bag into the small closet and sinking gratefully back onto her tiny mattress. 

"I am resurrected," Stella declared, her voice still a bit gravelly from sleep. She rubbed her stomach carefully. "The nausea is completely gone. My abdominal muscles feel like I did a thousand crunches, but I think the cheese has finally vacated the premises."

"Good, because you need to eat," Olivia said, gently depositing Leo back into his spot on the lower bunk. She reached into her own duffel bag and pulled out a sleeve of saltine crackers and a bottle of ginger ale she had bought before they boarded. "You're off the BRAT diet, but you're on the cracker protocol tonight. Small bites. We need to get some calories in you."

Stella didn't argue. She accepted the crackers and the soda, taking a cautious, grateful bite. "Thank you, Liv. Honestly, you've been a lifesaver today."

Olivia waved off the compliment, unscrewing the cap of a large water bottle. "Don't thank me yet. I'm about to become a tyrant again. Where are we?"

Luna leaned over and peered out the window into the pitch-black night. Occasional lights from distant farmhouses whipped past the glass. "I think we're almost to Omaha. We're about to cross into Nebraska."

"Perfect," Olivia said, handing the water bottle to Luna, then pulling out a second one with a straw for Leo. "We are about to start a massive, steady climb. Omaha is barely a thousand feet above sea level. By the time we wake up tomorrow, we'll be in Denver at over five thousand feet. I need everyone to drink a full bottle of water before we go to sleep to prep for the altitude."

Leo gladly took the straw. He always found it easier to sleep when he felt like there was a structured plan in place. Olivia’s clinical, take-charge attitude was exactly what the pack needed with Stella recovering. 

Up in the top bunk, Stella munched on her crackers, watching Olivia manage her siblings with a quiet, appreciative smile. She knew she could always rely on Luna for emotional support and meticulous routine management, but Olivia had the raw physical strength and the unshakeable, practical endurance that kept the fortress moving when things broke down. 

"Drink up, Stella," Olivia ordered, pointing a finger up at the top bunk. "You lost a lot of fluids yesterday. And the air on this train is only going to get thinner."

"Yes, ma'am," Stella chuckled, taking a sip of her ginger ale. 

The mood in the cabin had noticeably lightened. With everyone clean, dressed in soft clothes, and actively preparing for the geographic shift, the cramped dimensions of the Family Bedroom felt less like a prison and more like a cozy, rolling bunker. 

Leo settled deeper into his pillows on the Adult Lower bunk. A few minutes later, Olivia climbed in beside him, maneuvering her tall frame into the narrow space between him and the wall. She wrapped an arm over his chest, her steady breathing syncing up with the rhythmic *clack-clack-clack* of the train wheels against the steel tracks. 

Outside the window, the flat, dark expanse of the Great Plains rushed by as the *Western Pioneer* began its long, invisible climb into the Rocky Mountains.

## The Mile High City

### Part 1: The Mile High City

**Date:** 2003-06-27 at 06:30 America/Denver

Sometime during the deep, pitch-black hours of the night, while the *Western Pioneer* rattled across the high plains of western Nebraska, the train silently crossed a geographical threshold. They had officially left the Central Time Zone and entered Mountain Time. 

When Leo woke up in the cramped, narrow confines of the Adult Lower bunk, the first rays of morning light were just beginning to filter through the curtains. He was still wrapped tightly in Olivia's arms, her even breathing a steady rhythm against his back. 

He blinked, reaching awkwardly with a contracted arm to tap the button on his digital watch. The screen illuminated in the dim cabin: **8:30 AM**. 

Because it was a standard 2003 digital watch, it hadn't automatically updated. It was still anchored to the time in Virginia Beach. 

Leo stared at the glowing green numbers, doing the math in his head. They had crossed into Mountain Time overnight. It was only 6:30 AM locally. 

A sharp, familiar pang of anxiety immediately flared in his chest. Looking at the literal, mathematical difference between where he was and where he lived was a geographical reality check. It was undeniable proof of exactly how far West they had traveled, and a terrifying reminder that the Pacific Time Zone—and Olivia's departure—was looming just beyond the mountains. 

He let out a quiet, shuddering breath, trying to push the panic down. He shifted slightly, and Olivia's arms instantly tightened around him in her sleep, a subconscious, protective reflex. The deep pressure helped ground him, keeping the timezone anxiety at a manageable simmer. 

"Morning," a soft, raspy voice whispered from the top bunk. 

Leo looked up. Stella was peering over the edge of her mattress, her hair a messy bird's nest. She looked significantly better than she had the night before. The pallor was completely gone, and her eyes were bright and alert. The long, horizontal stretch of the Adult Upper bunk had done exactly what she needed it to do. 

<aac>How is your stomach,</aac> Leo typed on his communicator, making sure the volume was turned down low so he wouldn't wake Luna, who was still curled tightly in her fetal position in the child's bed across the aisle. 

"A hundred percent better," Stella whispered, carefully climbing down the small ladder. "The guardian bear has officially resumed her post."

She stretched her arms over her head, cracking her back, then reached over to peel back the heavy curtain covering the large window. 

The view outside was staggering. 

The flat, endless expanse of the Great Plains was gone. In its place, rising like a massive, impenetrable wall of dark rock and snow-capped peaks against the western horizon, were the Rocky Mountains. They were colossal, beautiful, and incredibly intimidating. 

"Wow," Stella breathed, staring out the window as the train began to slow. The sprawling suburban grid of Denver was beginning to rise up around the tracks, nestled right against the foothills of the mountain range. 

Olivia stirred behind Leo, letting out a sleepy yawn. She blinked her green eyes open, resting her chin on his shoulder to look out the window. "We made it," she murmured, her voice rough with sleep. "Denver."

"And we're at five thousand feet," Stella noted, rolling her shoulders. "I can definitely feel the air is thinner. It feels crisp."

Luna groaned from the tiny Child Lower bunk, slowly uncurling her stiff limbs. "I feel like a pretzel," she complained, rubbing her lower back as she sat up. "I don't think my spine is supposed to bend that way for eight hours."

"We'll get you stretched out as soon as we're off the train, Luna," Stella promised, reaching over to playfully ruffle her sister's messy hair. "We've got a two-day layover here. Lots of time to stretch."

A low chime echoed through the train's PA system, followed by the conductor's voice. *"Good morning, passengers. We are currently on approach to Denver Union Station. We anticipate an on-time arrival at 7:15 AM. Please make sure you have all your belongings..."*

The pack moved into action. The overnight train ride had been cramped, logistically brutal, and emotionally taxing, but they had survived it. Stella was back to full health, the twins had survived the altitude climb in their sleep, and the majestic, imposing wall of the Rocky Mountains was waiting for them just outside the station doors. 

Leo watched the mountains draw closer, the sheer scale of them making him feel incredibly small. The great journey West was officially entering its most beautiful, and most daunting, phase.

### Part 2: Denver Regional Transit

**Date:** 2003-06-27 at 07:30 America/Denver

Stepping off the *Western Pioneer* onto the concrete platform of Denver Union Station was a physical shock to the system. 

The air was noticeably thinner, incredibly crisp, and lacked the heavy, suffocating humidity they were used to on the East Coast. It felt clean, pulling sharply into their lungs with every breath. 

"Okay," Stella said, pausing on the platform and taking a deep breath of the mountain air. "I feel completely revived. The air here is amazing."

"It's dry," Luna noted, immediately digging into her duffel bag for a tube of chapstick. "But I'll take this over Chicago humidity any day."

Olivia pushed Leo's wheelchair along the platform, easily keeping pace with the flow of departing passengers. "So, what's the logistical plan, Commander? Do we have a shuttle?"

"Better," Stella grinned, pointing toward the end of the station complex. "We have the DRT."

The Denver Regional Transit (DRT) system was a sprawling network of buses and light rail trains that crisscrossed the city. More importantly, it was fully wheelchair accessible. 

For Leo, the prospect of taking a local commuter train was thrilling. It combined two of his favorite things: predictable rail travel and an environment built to accommodate his wheelchair without requiring a massive logistical struggle. 

They followed the clear signage out of the historic train station and over to the modern light rail platform. The morning commuter rush was just starting to build, a mix of business professionals in suits and locals in outdoor gear. 

<aac>This is very convenient,</aac> Leo typed on his communicator as they waited by the bright yellow tactile strip on the edge of the platform. 

"It really is," Stella agreed, checking the transit map she had printed out before they left Virginia. "Our hotel is just a few stops away on the main line. We don't have to worry about finding an accessible cab, and we don't have to deal with loading and unloading all this luggage into a trunk."

A few minutes later, a sleek, modern light rail train glided into the station with a quiet, electric hum. The doors hissed open, perfectly level with the platform. 

Olivia pushed Leo straight on board, parking his wheelchair in the designated accessible space near the doors. She locked his brakes and grabbed a nearby handrail, while Stella and Luna piled the luggage into the adjacent row of seats. 

The train doors closed, and the DRT accelerated smoothly out of the station. Through the large windows, the sprawling cityscape of Denver rolled by, backed by the ever-present, majestic wall of the Rocky Mountains. 

Leo watched the mountains pass, feeling the steady, rhythmic vibration of the light rail train beneath his wheels. The thin air was exhilarating, the transit system was flawless, and for the first time since they left Pittsburgh, the heavy dread of the ticking clock was pushed aside by genuine, unbridled excitement for the new city.

### Part 3: The Horizon Suites

**Date:** 2003-06-27 at 08:00 America/Denver

The DRT light rail carried them smoothly out of the downtown core and into a quieter commercial district. They got off at a modern, concrete station platform and rolled two blocks down the sidewalk to their destination: The Horizon Suites. 

After the opulent, historic grandeur of the Chicago hotel, The Horizon Suites was refreshingly mundane. It was a mid-tier budget hotel, the kind of modern, cookie-cutter building favored by traveling salespeople and frugal road-trippers. 

"I know it's not exactly five stars," Stella said, pushing open the glass front doors to the lobby. "But after I paid Chicago prices for deep dish pizza and Skydeck tickets, the fortress budget needed a breather."

"It's perfect," Olivia said, looking around the clean, brightly lit lobby. 

It wasn't luxurious, but it was top-of-the-line for a budget brand. Everything was new, the floors were spotless, and most importantly, the ADA accessibility was completely standardized. 

They checked in at the front desk and took the elevator up to the third floor. When Stella unlocked their room, they found a clean, spacious layout with two standard queen beds, a generic faux-wood desk, and a large, accessible bathroom equipped with grab bars and a roll-in shower. 

For Leo, the lack of historic charm was a massive feature, not a bug. Historic usually meant cramped, unpredictable, and full of architectural barriers. The Horizon Suites was predictable. He knew exactly where the light switches would be. He knew the bathroom would have a wide turning radius. The standardization was deeply soothing to his nervous system. 

"Okay, this is actually really nice," Luna said, dropping her duffel bag onto one of the beds and bouncing on the mattress to test it. "It's clean, and the TV is huge."

"And the bathroom is fully accessible," Olivia noted, peaking into the brightly lit washroom. "No more cramped ARTS closets for the next two days."

"Exactly," Stella smiled, dropping her own bags near the desk. She walked over to the window and pulled back the curtains. Their third-floor room didn't have a view of the mountains, but it looked out over a quiet, tree-lined street. "Alright, pack. We have a two-day layover. The first order of business is getting unpacked. The second order of business is taking a nap in a bed that isn't moving."

"And stretching," Luna groaned, rolling her shoulders. "I am going to do yoga on this carpet for an hour. My spine is still shaped like an ARTS child's bed."

Leo drove his wheelchair over to the window, parking next to Stella. The room felt incredibly safe. It was quiet, the air conditioning hummed with a steady white noise, and the deep pressure of the overnight train ride was fading. 

<aac>This is a good base camp,</aac> he typed on his communicator. 

"I'm glad you think so," Stella said, resting a hand affectionately on his shoulder. "We're going to take it easy today. We need to let our bodies adjust to the altitude before we do any serious sightseeing."

Leo nodded. He liked that plan. The ticking clock was still there, quietly counting down the hours until they had to board the train to California, but for now, they were safely anchored in a clean, quiet room a mile above sea level.

### Part 4: The MallRide

**Date:** 2003-06-27 at 17:00 America/Denver

By late afternoon, the pack had successfully napped, unpacked, and stretched out their various Amtrak-induced aches and pains. Stella's stomach was officially back to normal, and she was eager to get them out of the hotel room to enjoy the famously crisp Colorado evening. 

"Okay, I have a plan," Stella announced, unfurling a tourist map on one of the queen beds. "It's a beautiful Friday evening. We are going to head downtown to a district called LoDo. We can ride the shuttle, find a quiet place for dinner, and just enjoy the ground-level mountain air."

"Sounds perfect," Olivia smiled, grabbing her light jacket. "How do we get there?"

"That's the best part," Stella grinned. "We take the Free MallRide."

Ten minutes later, they were back out on the street. The Free MallRide was a fleet of distinctive, low-floor shuttle buses that ran constantly up and down the 16th Street pedestrian mall. Because they were designed to be incredibly easy to board, Leo didn't even have to use a complicated mechanical lift. The bus simply kneeled, and Olivia rolled him right on board. 

The shuttle was pleasantly full of locals and tourists enjoying the summer evening. Leo parked his wheelchair in the center bay, enjoying the cool breeze blowing through the open windows of the bus as it crawled past shops, restaurants, and street performers. 

"This is infinitely better than navigating East Coast traffic," Stella said, holding onto a hand strap as the bus glided along the pavement. 

As they neared the Lower Downtown (LoDo) district, the massive, red-brick structure of Summit Stadium loomed into view at the end of the street. It was a beautiful, towering building designed to look like a classic, old-school ballpark. 

"Wow," Luna breathed, looking out the bus window at the massive floodlights. "It's huge."

"That's where the Denver Peaks play," Stella noted, referencing her map. "They're actually playing an away series in Pittsburgh this weekend. Which is ironic, since we just came from there. Otherwise, I might have suggested we go to a game."

Leo looked out the window at the sheer scale of the massive, empty stadium. A sudden, undeniable wave of relief washed over him. As he imagined what it would look like full—forty thousand screaming people, blaring PA systems, and suffocating crowds—he realized his carefully regulated nervous system would have absolutely short-circuited. 

He glanced over at Luna. She was staring at the stadium, too, her shoulders visibly relaxing as she came to the exact same realization. 

"You know what?" Luna said, a small, grateful smile playing on her lips. "I think I'm okay with the team being in Pittsburgh. Forty thousand people sounds like a sensory nightmare."

<aac>Agreed,</aac> Leo typed, hitting the play button emphatically. 

Olivia chuckled, patting Leo on the shoulder as the bus slowed for their stop. "Alright, no baseball for the fortress. Let's just go find some dinner."

### Part 5: Bison and Oysters

**Date:** 2003-06-27 at 18:00 America/Denver

The pack got off the Free MallRide in the heart of LoDo, the historic Lower Downtown district of Denver. The streets were lined with beautifully restored Victorian brick buildings that had been converted into lofts, galleries, and restaurants. 

"Okay, so what is traditional Colorado food?" Luna asked, looking around at the various awnings. "I feel like we should eat something authentic."

"Well, there's always Rocky Mountain Oysters," Olivia suggested, pointing to a rustic-looking tavern across the street with a menu posted in the window. 

Leo wrinkled his nose. He liked some seafood, but oysters seemed a bit slimy. <aac>Oysters in the mountains?</aac> he typed, eyeing Olivia skeptically. The ocean was a thousand miles away. 

Olivia grinned, a mischievous glint in her green eyes. "Oh, they aren't from the ocean, Leo. They're bull testicles. Deep fried."

Olivia meant it as a lighthearted, gross-out joke, expecting a theatrical gag from the twins. But she miscalculated. 

Because of his profound intellectual disability, Leo did not understand sex or reproductive anatomy. What he did understand—what he remembered with vivid, terrifying clarity—were the crude, vicious taunts of middle school bullies like Josh Riley. Boys who would corner him, using biological words like weapons to humiliate him and make him feel small, broken, and unsafe. 

Hearing that specific word didn't just gross him out; it triggered a deep, visceral spike of panic. 

Leo's hand dropped away from his communicator. His breathing hitched, shallow and fast. He stared at the tavern window, his eyes wide and unblinking, his body suddenly rigid as the phantom echoes of locker-room bullying flooded his nervous system. 

Olivia's smile vanished instantly. She saw his chest seizing, saw the raw, unadulterated fear replacing the curiosity in his eyes, and she immediately realized her mistake. 

"Hey. Hey, Leo, look at me," Olivia said, her voice dropping into a low, firm, incredibly gentle register. She stepped right in front of his wheelchair, blocking his view of the tavern window, and placed both of her strong hands firmly on his shoulders. 

He flinched slightly, but her grip was steady and safe. 

"I'm sorry," she said clearly, holding his gaze. "That was a stupid joke. We aren't eating that. We aren't going in there. You're safe. I've got you."

She applied deep, downward pressure on his shoulders, forcing his highly sensitive nervous system to process the grounding physical contact. 

Luna stepped up beside him, her own disgust instantly evaporating as she recognized her twin's distress. She placed a hand over his on the armrest. "We're going to get burgers," Luna promised softly. "Just normal, boring burgers."

Leo let out a long, shuddering breath, his chest rising and falling heavily as the panic slowly receded. He looked at Olivia's hands, then up at her face. There was no teasing there—only fierce, protective focus. He nodded once, slowly. 

"Okay," Stella said quietly, stepping in to cleanly break the tension. "I just survived the Chicago deep dish incident anyway. I am finding us a nice, normal burger place right now."

They eventually settled on a lively, accessible brewpub a few blocks down that specialized in another Colorado staple: Bison burgers. 

The pub had a wide, flat entrance and plenty of space between the heavy wooden tables, allowing Olivia to park Leo comfortably at the end of a booth. 

When the food arrived, it was hearty and deeply satisfying. The bison meat was leaner and slightly sweeter than regular beef, served on thick, toasted buns with crispy fries. Stella, still playing it safe with her stomach, had opted for a simple grilled chicken sandwich, but she looked genuinely happy as she ate. 

"This is amazing," Luna mumbled through a mouthful of bison burger. "Way better than the oysters."

Leo took a bite of the burger Olivia had cut up for him. It was delicious. The lively, conversational hum of the pub was a perfect volume—enough background noise to feel energetic, but nowhere near the overwhelming roar of a stadium. 

He looked around the table. Stella was laughing at a joke Olivia had just made, and Luna was happily dipping her fries in ketchup. The crisp mountain air blowing in through the open front doors felt clean and refreshing. 

The timezone shift was still a looming reality, and the ticking clock was still quietly counting down the days until Seattle. But here, eating bison burgers in a brick tavern in the shadow of the Rocky Mountains, the fortress felt incredibly strong. For the first time since they left Virginia, Leo felt like they were actually on an adventure, rather than just surviving a geographical relocation.

## The Botanic Gardens

### Part 1: The Centennial Botanic Gardens

**Date:** 2003-06-28 at 10:00 America/Denver

The next morning, Stella announced a strict, low-exertion itinerary. They had survived the timezone anxiety, the cramped ARTS closet, and the near-miss at Summit Stadium, but they still needed to let their bodies fully acclimate to the 5,280-foot elevation. 

"We are going to be outside, but we are not hiking," Stella declared, making sure everyone had a full bottle of water before they left the hotel room. "We are going to find flat ground, paved paths, and lots of shade. We are going to the Centennial Botanic Gardens."

After another easy ride on the DRT, the pack arrived at the massive, sprawling urban oasis of the Centennial Botanic Gardens. 

It was exactly what they needed. The gardens were a labyrinth of flawlessly paved, ADA-accessible paths winding through dozens of specialized, highly manicured biomes. It was the exact opposite of the chaotic sensory nightmare of a baseball stadium. It was quiet, fragrant, and profoundly peaceful. 

Olivia pushed Leo's wheelchair along a wide concrete path that snaked through a massive alpine garden, past cascading water features and delicate, brightly colored mountain flowers. The crisp, dry mountain air felt incredibly clean, and the sun was warm on their shoulders. 

"This is amazing," Luna said, stopping to inspect a patch of vivid purple columbines. "It feels like we're in the mountains, but I don't have to climb anything."

"That's the magic of Denver," Olivia smiled, resting her hands lightly on the push handles of Leo's chair. "You get all the scenery with half the effort."

Leo let his head rest back against his headrest, closing his eyes for a moment as they rolled into the dappled shade of a massive cottonwood tree. The deep, rumbling stress of the long train ride was finally melting out of his muscles. The environment here was perfectly structured. There were clear paths to follow, quiet places to sit, and no sudden, loud noises to startle him. 

<aac>This is very nice,</aac> he typed on his communicator, turning the volume up just enough to cut through the quiet splashing of a nearby fountain. 

"I'm glad you like it, buddy," Stella said, dropping onto a wooden bench near the fountain and taking a long drink of water. She looked completely relaxed, the guardian bear finally able to drop her defensive posture in the safe, enclosed environment. 

They spent the next two hours slowly winding their way through the massive complex. They explored a climate-controlled tropical conservatory—a massive glass pyramid that felt comfortably warm—and wandered through a serene, carefully raked Japanese garden. 

For the first time since they left Virginia Beach, the ticking clock in Leo's mind fell completely silent. 

He didn't think about the fact that they were in the Mountain Time Zone. He didn't think about the terrifying Pacific Time Zone waiting for them across the Rockies. He just sat in his chair, feeling the sun on his face, listening to Luna happily identify different types of ferns, and letting Olivia push him safely through the beautiful, highly structured world. 

It was a perfectly functional, joyous, and fiercely protective display of the fortress operating at peak efficiency.

### Part 2: Thin Air

**Date:** 2003-06-28 at 14:00 America/Denver

Despite the peaceful, low-exertion environment of the Botanic Gardens, the geographical reality of the Mile High City eventually caught up with them. 

By early afternoon, the physical toll of being 5,280 feet above sea level began to manifest in the twins. Their carefully regulated bodies, built and conditioned for the dense, humid air of the East Coast sea level, were struggling to process the thin, dry oxygen of the Rockies. 

Leo felt a dull, persistent throbbing at the base of his skull. No matter how much water Olivia made him drink through his straw, his mouth felt like cotton, and an overwhelming wave of lethargy made it difficult to even keep his eyes open. 

Beside him on a shaded bench, Luna wasn't faring any better. She had abandoned her enthusiastic identification of local flora and was currently resting her head on her knees, her eyes closed. 

"My head hurts," Luna mumbled, her voice muffled against her jeans. "And I feel like I just ran a marathon, but all we did was walk past some orchids."

Stella frowned, crouching down to press the back of her hand against Luna's forehead. "No fever. Just altitude sickness. You guys are dehydrated, and your bodies are working overtime just to pull oxygen into your blood."

<aac>This sucks,</aac> Leo typed slowly, his finger feeling heavy and uncoordinated. 

"I know, buddy," Olivia sighed, rubbing the back of his neck gently. "I can feel it too. The air is just too thin for our East Coast lungs."

Stella stood up, making a swift, executive decision. The guardian bear was back in full logistical mode. "Alright. We are cutting the Denver layover short. We aren't doing another day of this."

"Can we change the train tickets?" Olivia asked, looking concerned. "We were supposed to stop in Salt Lake City on the way to California."

"Salt Lake City is over four thousand feet," Stella countered, shaking her head. "It's basically the same altitude as here. If they're struggling in Denver, they are going to struggle in Utah, and the train doesn't even get into Salt Lake until almost midnight. We aren't doing it. I'll call ARTS when we get back to the hotel. We're going to bypass Utah entirely."

"Where are we going?" Luna asked weakly, finally lifting her head. 

"We are booking a straight-through sleeper cabin," Stella said firmly. "We are going to ride the train up over the Rockies, across the Nevada desert, and straight down into Emeryville. We are getting you guys back to sea level as fast as geographically possible."

Leo felt a massive wave of relief wash over him. The thought of getting back to sea level—to dense, breathable air and the familiar smell of the ocean—sounded like heaven. He didn't care that they were skipping Salt Lake City. He just wanted to stop feeling like he was suffocating in slow motion. 

"Let's get back to the hotel," Olivia said, unlocking the brakes on Leo's wheelchair. "We'll get you guys into bed with the AC running while Stella works her magic on the phone."

The pack moved out of the gardens with renewed, singular purpose. The Rocky Mountains were beautiful, but the fortress had officially reached its vertical limit. It was time to head back to the coast.

### Part 3: One More Night

**Date:** 2003-06-28 at 15:00 America/Denver

Stella was on her cell phone the moment they got back to the quiet, air-conditioned sanctuary of their room at The Horizon Suites. 

Olivia quickly helped Leo transfer out of his wheelchair and into one of the queen beds, propping him up with pillows so he could rest his aching head. Luna had immediately face-planted onto the other bed, asleep almost before her head hit the mattress. 

"Yes, the Western Pioneer," Stella said into her phone, pacing near the window. "I need to change our itinerary. We want to cancel the stopover in Salt Lake City and book straight through to Emeryville. Four passengers, one wheelchair accessible... yes, the Family Bedroom again if you have it."

Leo closed his eyes, focusing on the deep, rhythmic hum of the hotel's air conditioning unit. The cool air was a massive relief after the bright sun of the Botanic Gardens. 

"Wait, 8:05 AM?" Stella's voice spiked in surprise. She checked her watch. "There's only one train a day? Okay. No, that's fine. We'll take it tomorrow morning."

She finished the transaction, gave the agent her confirmation number, and flipped her cell phone shut with a sharp snap. 

"Good news and bad news," Stella announced, turning back to the room. 

Olivia looked up from where she was pulling a blanket over Leo's legs. "Give me the bad news first."
