"The bad news is that the westbound ARTS train leaves Denver precisely at 8:05 in the morning," Stella said, rubbing her temples. "Which means we've completely missed it for today. We are stuck at five thousand feet for one more night."

Leo groaned softly from his pile of pillows. 

"And the good news?" Olivia asked, gently brushing the hair out of Leo's eyes. 

"The good news is that I got us the Family Bedroom all the way to California starting tomorrow morning," Stella grinned, waving her phone like a trophy. "We don't have to get off in Utah. Once we board that train tomorrow, we are on a straight shot back to sea level. The air is going to get thicker by the hour."

"Thank God," Olivia sighed. "We need to get out of the mountains."

"So, here is the new itinerary," Stella declared, walking over to pull the heavy blackout curtains closed over the window, plunging the room into a cool, soothing darkness. "We are officially in survival mode. Nobody moves. Nobody exerts themselves. We lie in these beds, we drink water, and we order room service until it is time to get on the DRT tomorrow morning."

<aac>Best itinerary ever,</aac> Leo typed, the text-to-speech voice sounding appropriately enthusiastic in the dark room. 

"I thought you'd like that," Stella chuckled, kicking off her shoes and climbing into the bed next to the sleeping Luna. 

Leo settled back into his pillows, feeling Olivia crawl into the bed beside him. She wrapped an arm around his waist, grounding him with the familiar, heavy pressure he needed. The dull ache in his head was still there, but knowing that they were leaving tomorrow—that they were abandoning the thin air and heading for the ocean—made it bearable. 

They were going to survive one more night in the Mile High City.

## The Moffat Tunnel

### Part 1: Night Terrors

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 02:00 America/Denver

When the time finally came to officially go to sleep, the pack naturally gravitated toward their established, unspoken formations. Even though there were two spacious queen beds in the hotel room, Luna didn't want to sleep alone. She climbed into the bed where Leo was resting, squeezing herself onto the mattress so that Leo was sandwiched securely between her and Olivia. 

It was a tight fit, but the deep, grounding pressure of having a warm body on either side of him was exactly what Leo's exhausted, altitude-stressed nervous system needed to finally shut down. 

Sometime around 2:00 AM, however, the exhaustion curdled into a nightmare. 

It wasn't a normal dream. It was a visceral, suffocating night terror—a delayed psychological reaction to the massive influx of stress, the timezone shifts, and the crude, triggering joke outside the tavern. In the dark theater of his sleeping mind, Leo was surrounded. Faceless, aggressive shapes were closing in on him, grabbing at his chair, taunting him. He was trapped, unable to speak, unable to defend himself. 

In the real world, Leo let out a sharp, choked gasp. He rolled violently onto his stomach, his contracted arms suddenly lashing out in blind, panicked swings against the mattress as he fought desperately against the invisible attackers. 

"Leo?" Olivia's groggy voice cut through the dark. She sat up instantly, alarmed by the sudden violence of his thrashing. "Leo, hey, wake—"

She didn't get to finish the sentence. 

Before Olivia could even fully orient herself, Luna was already moving. Operating on pure, deeply ingrained muscle memory, the eighteen-year-old girl scrambled over the tangled bedsheets. She didn't hesitate or try to gently wake him up. Instead, she threw her entire body directly on top of him. 

Luna landed face-down on his back. She instantly locked her long legs around his waist and snaked her arms tightly under his thrashing ones, wrapping her arms completely around his torso. With a fierce, practiced motion, she clamped her arms shut, brutally pinning his swinging arms flush against his own sides. She buried her face firmly into the crook of his neck, effectively turning herself into a human straitjacket. 

Olivia lunged forward, her protective instincts flaring. "Luna, what are you doing? Let him—"

"Stand down, Liv!" Stella's voice snapped sharply from the other bed. 

The bedside lamp clicked on, flooding the room with dull yellow light. Stella was sitting up, her eyes wide but her posture completely calm and authoritative. 

Olivia froze, her hands hovering inches from Luna's back. "He's having a seizure. She's crushing him!"

"He's not seizing, he's having a night terror," Stella corrected, her voice steady and commanding. "If you try to wake him up now, he'll accidentally hit you, or he'll hurt himself. Luna knows exactly what she's doing. She's been pulling him out of these for years. Just stand down and give her a minute."

Olivia slowly lowered her hands, her chest heaving as she watched the chaotic scene on the mattress. 

Underneath the crushing, immovable weight of his twin sister, Leo was still fighting. His lungs burned as he tried to buck her off, his mind still trapped in the terrifying hallucination. But Luna didn't budge. She held onto him with a fierce, desperate strength, her body providing a massive, inescapable wave of deep proprioceptive pressure. 

"I've got you," Luna whispered fiercely into his ear, her voice tight with exertion. "I'm right here. You're safe. I've got you, Leo. Come back to me."

Slowly, the heavy, grounding weight of his twin sister began to break through the panic. The invisible attackers in his mind started to dissolve, replaced by the physical reality of the mattress beneath him and Luna's familiar, rhythmic breathing against his neck. 

The frantic thrashing began to subside into violent tremors, and then, finally, into heavy, exhausted sobs. 

Leo went limp against the mattress. He was awake. He was safe. 

"There you go," Luna murmured. 

But she didn't let go, and she didn't climb off of him. As the fight left his body, the brutal, clinical restraint seamlessly transitioned into a profound, prolonged hug. She simply stayed lying face down on top of him, adjusting her arms so they were wrapped lovingly around his torso rather than pinning him. 

Olivia watched, still hovering near the edge of the bed. "Is she going to get up?" she whispered to Stella. "She's squishing him."

"No, she's not. Leave them be," Stella said softly, leaning back against her own headboard. "That's the second part of the protocol. Once the terror breaks, it turns into a hug. She'll stay on top of him until she is absolutely satisfied that he's fully back to reality. And then she'll probably just stay there because they both like it."

Olivia looked at Stella, awe mixing with the lingering adrenaline. "Have you ever...?"

"Tried to pull him out of one?" Stella nodded slowly. "Yeah. Plenty of times. But I don't have the magic touch. Luna mastered the technique years ago. She knows exactly how much pressure to apply." Stella offered a small, bittersweet smile. "Plus, when his world has completely fallen apart and he's terrified, he'd much rather realize his twin sister is the one holding him together than his older sister."

Olivia watched the two of them, the steady rise and fall of their backs moving in unison. "Does she do this every time?"

"Every time," Stella confirmed quietly. "And if Luna ever has an autistic meltdown or a terror of her own, Leo does the exact same thing for her. They are each other's weighted blankets. It's how they survive."

Olivia sat silently on the edge of the mattress, the last of her adrenaline fading into a profound realization. She possessed the raw, athletic strength to physically carry Leo across the country, but in the deepest, darkest moments of his psychological terror, it was Luna—the seemingly fragile, anxious twin—who was his ultimate, unbreakable shield.

### Part 2: The Western Pioneer

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 07:00 America/Denver

The sun rose over the Mile High City, pouring bright, harsh light through the gap in the blackout curtains. 

Leo was already awake. He was still lying on his stomach, completely pinned to the mattress by Luna. She hadn't moved all night. She was still draped heavily across his back, her legs tangled with his and her arms securely wrapped around his torso. 

He didn't try to wake her. The crushing pressure of his twin sister felt like a physical anchor tethering him to the real world, keeping the dark, chaotic terrors of the night firmly locked away. 

Eventually, Stella’s alarm went off across the room. 

"Alright, fortress, time to move," Stella groaned, slapping the alarm clock on the nightstand. "We have an eight o'clock train to catch."

Luna stirred, letting out a muffled sigh against Leo's neck. She slowly unhooked her arms and rolled off of him, rubbing her eyes behind her glasses. She didn't say anything about the night terror, and neither did he. It was an unspoken, lifelong protocol. She had pulled him out of the darkness, and now it was morning. 

Olivia quickly helped Leo transfer back into his motorized wheelchair. He still had a lingering headache from the altitude, but it was vastly overshadowed by the adrenaline of knowing they were finally leaving the thin air behind. 

They packed up their bags, checked out of The Horizon Suites, and rolled quickly down the 16th Street pedestrian mall to Union Station. 

The historic, Beaux-Arts train station was bustling with morning commuters, but the pack moved with practiced, military precision. Stella led the way, brandishing their updated tickets like a shield. They bypassed the crowds, navigating the accessible ramps directly to the boarding platform where the massive, silver Superliner cars of the *Western Pioneer* were waiting. 

"Family Bedroom, straight through to Emeryville," the conductor announced, checking Stella's tickets and lowering the metal bridge to the train. "You folks are in for a spectacular ride today. We're going straight through the Rockies."

Olivia pushed Leo onto the lower level of the sleeping car, navigating the tight corner into the familiar, cramped space of the Family Bedroom. 

It was exactly the same claustrophobic layout they had endured across Nebraska—two tiny adult bunks on one side, two child-sized bunks on the other, spanning the entire width of the train car with windows on both sides. But this time, nobody complained about the lack of space. 

"Okay, beds are stowed, bags are under the seats," Stella said, dropping heavily onto the lower couch and letting out a long breath. "We did it. We survived Denver."

At exactly 8:05 AM, the massive diesel engines of the *Western Pioneer* roared to life. The train gave a violent, metallic lurch, and then slowly began to pull away from the concrete platform of Union Station. 

Leo sat perfectly still in the narrow space between the bunks, watching the brick buildings of downtown Denver slowly begin to slide backward past the window. 

As the train picked up speed, the towering, snow-capped peaks of the Rocky Mountains filled the forward windows. It was a massive, intimidating wall of rock that seemed to entirely block the horizon. 

They were officially leaving the Midwest behind. The final, massive leg of their cross-country journey had begun.

### Part 3: The Climb to the Divide

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 10:00 America/Denver

The *Western Pioneer* didn't just glide out of Denver; it fought for every inch of altitude. 

For the first hour, the massive double-decker train aggressively wound its way up the sheer face of the Front Range. The tracks bent into impossible, sweeping horseshoes—the infamous "Big Ten Curve"—where Leo could look out his window and see the very back of their own train trailing far below them. The urban sprawl of Denver shrank into a hazy grid on the plains, replaced by towering walls of granite and dense, dark pine forests.

But as the train climbed higher into the sky, the air inside the sleeper car began to change. 

Denver had been a mile high. By 10:00 AM, the train was approaching nine thousand feet above sea level. 

Leo was sitting on the edge of the lower bunk, staring out the window, when the first wave of lethargy hit him. It felt like an invisible weight pressing down on his chest. His breathing grew shallow and rapid, his lungs instinctively trying to pull oxygen out of air that was growing aggressively thin. 

Across the tiny compartment, Luna was slumped against the wall, her eyes half-closed and her chest rising and falling in quick, uneven hitches. 

"Okay, oxygen check," Stella announced, her voice cutting through the heavy hum of the diesel engines. She was sitting in the corner jump seat, completely unaffected by the altitude. 

Olivia immediately dropped her book and moved into action. She leaned over Luna first, checking her pulse and gently tilting her sister's head back. "Deep breaths, Lu. In through your nose, out through your mouth. You're just panting. You have to pull the air all the way down."

Luna reached clumsily for her own NovaVox resting beside her. She tapped a single, pre-programmed button, lacking the breath and focus to type out a full sentence. 

<aac>I'm trying. It feels empty,</aac> her device announced, the calm, synthesized female voice contrasting sharply with Luna's erratic, wheezing breaths. 

"It's just the elevation," Olivia promised, rubbing Luna's shoulders with a steady, grounding pressure. "We're almost at the peak. Once we cross the divide, we start going back down."

Stella shifted her attention to Leo. She didn't ask how he was feeling; she could see the pale, grayish tint creeping into his cheeks. She reached into her medical bag and pulled out a small, portable pulse oximeter, clipping it smoothly onto his index finger. 

The little digital screen blinked to life. *88%.*

"Yeah, you're not loving this mountain, are you?" Stella murmured, unzipping a secondary compartment in her bag. "Just relax, Leo. Don't fight it. Let your body figure it out." 

She pulled out a small, pressurized oxygen canister—a standard precaution she had bought at a pharmacy in Denver specifically for this leg of the trip. She fitted the plastic mask over Leo's mouth and nose, pressing the trigger. 

A rush of cool, concentrated oxygen flooded his lungs. The relief was instantaneous. The crushing weight on his chest lifted, and the dark edges of his vision cleared. He took a long, deep breath, the plastic mask fogging up with his exhalation. 

"Better?" Stella asked. 

Leo nodded weakly, keeping the mask pressed to his face. He reached out and pulled his NovaVox—his dedicated AAC tablet—onto his knees on the edge of the bunk. His fingers moved quickly over the thick, rugged touchscreen. 

<aac>Where are we going?</aac> the synthesized voice from the NovaVox asked, cutting through the low rumble of the train. <aac>After the mountains.</aac>

"We're on this train until tomorrow afternoon," Stella said, checking his oxygen levels again as they ticked back up into the high nineties. "Non-stop to Emeryville. Then we catch a bus across the bay into San Francisco."

Leo's fingers tapped the screen again. *Emeryville. Is that near Jack London Square?*

Olivia looked up from Luna, surprised. "Yeah, it is. How do you know about Jack London Square?"

*We read The Call of the Wild in middle school,* the electronic voice replied. Leo looked back out the window at the rugged, unforgiving wilderness passing by. *Mr. Harrison told us about him. The author. He said Jack London loved the cold. He loved the wild. I always wondered what his city looked like.*

Before Olivia could answer, the bright, mid-morning sun vanished. 

The train plunged into absolute, pitch-black darkness. The sudden absence of light was so absolute it felt physical. The rhythmic clatter of the wheels violently echoed off close, invisible stone walls. 

"Moffat Tunnel," Stella announced in the dark, her voice echoing in the cramped compartment. "Six point two miles straight through the Continental Divide. We're at the top of the world, kids." 

Leo sat in the dark, the oxygen mask hissing softly against his face. He felt the immense, millions of tons of solid mountain pressing down on the roof of the train. He thought about wolves, and wilderness, and escaping into the cold. 

For the first time in his life, he was going to see the ocean on the other side.

### Part 4: The Canyons of the Colorado

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 12:30 America/Denver

The darkness inside the Moffat Tunnel felt like it lasted a lifetime. 

For six long miles, the *Western Pioneer* rumbled through the heart of the Continental Divide, completely cut off from the outside world. Leo sat quietly in the lower bunk, his oxygen mask still strapped to his face, listening to the rhythmic, echoing clatter of the train wheels against the solid stone walls. Next to him, Luna's breathing had finally steadied into a normal rhythm, the initial shock of the extreme altitude slowly wearing off.

And then, just as suddenly as the sun had vanished, the train burst back into the light. 

The transition was violent and breathtaking. They didn't emerge into another dark pine forest or a sprawling plain. They shot out of the rock face and immediately found themselves clinging to the side of a massive, sun-drenched gorge. 

"Whoa," Olivia breathed, pressing her forehead directly against the glass. 

They were deep inside the Rocky Mountains, descending into Gore Canyon. The walls of the canyon were steep, jagged cliffs of red and gold rock that plummeted straight down. At the very bottom of the gorge, a raging river carved its way through the stone—the mighty Colorado River, winding its way west. 

The train tracks hugged the very edge of the water, tracing the impossible, winding path of the river canyon.

Stella unclipped the pulse oximeter from Leo's finger. The screen read a comfortable 98%. "Alright, oxygen mask comes off. We're back below eight thousand feet and dropping fast."

Leo reached up and pulled the plastic elastic band over his head, taking a deep, unfiltered breath. The air still felt incredibly fresh, but the crushing weight on his chest was completely gone. 

He looked over at his twin sister. Luna was leaning over Olivia's shoulder, completely mesmerized by the churning whitewater rapids of the Colorado River just a few dozen feet outside their window. She reached for her NovaVox, her fingers tapping out a quick, excited thought. 

<aac>It looks like another planet,</aac> the calm, synthesized female voice noted. 

Leo smiled, pulling his own NovaVox onto his lap. <aac>It looks like freedom,</aac> his device replied.

"It looks like it's time for lunch," Stella declared, standing up and stretching her arms over her head. The low ceiling of the Family Bedroom meant she couldn't stand up completely straight, but she didn't seem to mind. "They just called for the first dining car service. We have a reservation in fifteen minutes. And the best part is, we don't even have to leave this canyon to eat."

The train tilted aggressively into another tight curve, following the contour of the river. The sun caught the mist rising off the rapids, creating brief, shimmering rainbows against the red canyon walls. 

Leo secured his NovaVox in his lap, feeling a profound sense of peace settle over him. The terrifying darkness of the tunnel was behind them, and the vast, golden expanse of the American West was pulling them inexorably toward the ocean.

### Part 5: The Observation Deck

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 12:45 America/Denver

The Family Bedroom was located on the lower level of the sleeper car. To get to the dining car, they had to go upstairs and cross through two swaying, rattling train carriages. 

Earlier that morning, when the Lead Service Attendant had walked through the car to take lunch reservations, she had noticed Leo's folded wheelchair and politely offered to have their meals delivered directly to the room on trays. It was standard protocol for passengers with mobility issues. But Leo had reached for his NovaVox and typed out a quick question to Olivia: <aac>Will you carry me?</aac>

Olivia hadn't even hesitated. *Absolutely,* she had smiled. Stella had immediately turned back to the attendant and booked a table for four at 12:45. 

"Alright, wheelchair stays here," Olivia said now, cracking her knuckles as the time arrived. She stepped into the narrow space between the bunks and slid her arms securely under Leo's back and behind his knees. With a smooth, practiced deadlift, she hoisted him effortlessly into her arms. 

As she lifted him, Leo leaned heavily into the carry. He wrapped his arms securely around her neck, pulling himself into a tight, affectionate hug against her chest. He had complete trust in her. She carried him around their small, single-story house in Virginia all the time; a moving train was just a fun new balancing challenge. 

"Lead the way, Captain," Olivia said, shifting Leo's weight against her chest to keep her center of gravity low. 

Stella opened the sliding door and stepped out into the narrow corridor, followed closely by Luna, who was carrying both of their NovaVox tablets. 

They climbed the narrow, twisting staircase to the upper level of the sleeper car. The train was aggressively swaying side-to-side as it carved through the winding canyon, but Olivia’s footing was absolute. She moved with the sway of the train, absorbing the shocks through her legs, carrying Leo as easily as if he weighed nothing at all. 

They crossed through the heavy, sliding metal doors connecting the carriages, stepping over the loud, rattling metal plates that bridged the gap between the cars. 

"Whoa," Luna breathed, stopping dead in her tracks as they entered the next carriage. 

It was the Sightseer Lounge—the observation deck. The entire upper half of the train car was made of curved glass. Massive windows stretched from the floor, curving seamlessly into the ceiling, offering a completely unobstructed, panoramic view of the world outside. 

It felt like floating in a glass bubble directly over the Colorado River. The sheer, red-rock cliffs of Gore Canyon towered above them, practically close enough to touch, while the whitewater rapids raged directly below. 

"We'll come back here after we eat," Stella promised, gently steering Luna forward through the lounge. 

They crossed into the next car, which was filled with the clatter of silverware and the smell of roasting meat. The dining car was packed with passengers sitting at four-person booths with crisp white tablecloths. 

"Reservation for Porter," Stella said, handing a little blue paper slip to the attendant at the door. 

"Right this way," the attendant smiled, leading them to an empty booth right next to a massive window. 

Olivia slid into the booth first, gently depositing Leo onto the padded bench beside her before sliding out to let Luna sit next to him. 

"Okay, this is officially the coolest restaurant I've ever been to," Olivia grinned, grabbing a menu. 

Leo looked out the window. The train was still hugging the edge of the river, carving its way deeper into the American West. He couldn't walk, and he couldn't navigate the heavy doors of the train cars on his own, but sitting here in the dining car, completely surrounded by his pack, he didn't feel trapped at all. He felt like he was flying.

### Part 6: Dining Car Service

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 12:50 America/Denver

A sharply dressed waiter in a crisp white shirt and dark vest appeared at their table, placing four heavy, blue-rimmed ARTS menus down on the white tablecloth. 

"Welcome to the *Western Pioneer* dining car," he said with a warm smile, bracing himself against the edge of the booth as the train leaned heavily into another curve along the Colorado River. "Can I start you folks off with something to drink?"

"Four Cokes, please," Stella said, sliding her menu across the table. "And we actually already know what we want."

The waiter pulled out a small notepad. "Excellent. What can I get for you?"

"I'll take the grilled chicken sandwich," Stella said. 

"I'll do the same," Olivia added. 

Stella then pointed to the twins sitting across from her. "And for these two, we'll take two of the Angus Cheeseburgers. But here is the critical part: they need to be made exactly right. Meat, cheese, ketchup, mayo, lettuce, and bacon. Absolutely no tomatoes, no onions, no pickles, no mustard, and no relish anywhere near the plate. Just those safe ingredients and a side of fries."

The waiter didn't miss a beat. He jotted it down smoothly. "Two Angus burgers. Meat, cheese, bacon, lettuce, ketchup, and mayo. No garden surprises. We get specific orders all the time. I'll have those right out."

Leo watched the waiter disappear down the narrow aisle of the swaying car. He reached for his NovaVox, tapping out a quick message. 

<aac>Thank you,</aac> the device spoke, the synthesized voice directed across the table at Stella. He hated having to explain his sensory aversions to strangers, and he loved that Stella always aggressively intercepted those conversations before they even started. 

"Don't thank me yet," Stella chuckled, picking up her water glass. "If they bring out a single onion on that plate, I'm throwing it straight out the window into the river."

Luna grinned, resting her chin on her hands as she looked out the massive dining car window. The sheer, red rock walls of Gore Canyon were still blurring past them. It felt incredibly surreal to be sitting in a nice restaurant, drinking an ice-cold Coke, while hurtling through an untamed wilderness at fifty miles an hour. 

Olivia wrapped an arm loosely around Leo's shoulders, pulling him into a sideways hug in the booth. "You holding up okay, Captain?"

Leo leaned into her side, nodding happily. The altitude sickness was completely gone. He was surrounded by his favorite people in the world, eating his favorite safe food, on a train bound for the ocean. 

He didn't think life could get much better than this.

### Part 7: The Utah Border

**Date:** 2003-06-29 at 18:30 America/Denver

The transition out of the Rocky Mountains was just as dramatic as the climb into them. 

By early evening, the thick, towering pine forests and sheer granite cliffs of Colorado had completely vanished. The *Western Pioneer* was now carving its way through Ruby Canyon, winding alongside the Colorado River as it approached the Utah border. The landscape had violently shifted into a sprawling, arid desert of towering red sandstone mesas and deep, dry ravines. 

As the sun began to dip below the horizon, it cast long, dramatic shadows across the red rocks, painting the entire canyon in brilliant shades of burnt orange, gold, and deep violet. 

The pack had migrated back to the Family Bedroom on the lower level. Leo was sitting on the floor between the bunks, his back resting comfortably against Olivia’s legs as she sat on the lower couch. She was idly running her hand through his messy brown hair, a quiet, rhythmic gesture that kept him deeply grounded. 

Luna was lying on her stomach on the opposite bunk, her chin propped on her hands as she stared out the massive window at the alien, Martian-like landscape. 

"It's so empty out here," Luna murmured, her voice quiet in the small space. 

"It's the desert, Lu," Stella said, looking up from a paperback book she was reading under the small reading light. "We're about to cross into Utah. Once the sun goes down, it's just going to be hundreds of miles of absolute nothingness until we hit Nevada."

Leo looked out the window. The sky was burning with the intense colors of a desert sunset. It was beautiful, but it was also profoundly isolating. The immense scale of the American West was finally sinking in. They were thousands of miles away from their small, cozy house in Virginia, away from the dense, humid forests of the East Coast, away from the only world they had ever known. 

He reached for his NovaVox, tapping the thick screen. 

*Are there any cities out here?* he asked. 

"Not really," Olivia answered, resting her chin on the top of his head. "We stop in Salt Lake City in a few hours, but it'll be pitch black by then. And then we cross the salt flats in the middle of the night. You'll probably sleep through the whole thing."

Leo shivered slightly, despite the comfortable, climate-controlled air of the sleeper car. The thought of hurtling through a vast, pitch-black desert while everyone slept was unsettling. The ocean felt unimaginably far away. 

But then Olivia's hand resumed its gentle rhythm in his hair, and he looked across the narrow room at Stella and Luna. They weren't out in the desert. They were right here, safely enclosed in their rolling fortress of steel and glass. 

<aac>I'm glad we're together,</aac> his NovaVox spoke, the electronic voice cutting through the ambient hum of the train. 

Stella smiled, reaching across the small aisle to squeeze his shoulder. "Always, kiddo. The fortress moves with us."

## The Salt Lake Stop

### Part 1: The Salt Lake Stop

**Date:** 2003-06-30 at 00:30 America/Denver

The *Western Pioneer* was running almost an hour behind schedule, which meant it didn't pull into Utah's capital until well past midnight. 

Leo was lying on his back in the lower bunk of the Family Bedroom. Olivia was lying next to him on the narrow mattress, one arm thrown heavily across his chest to keep him physically grounded in his sleep. The rhythm of the train usually kept him under, but the sudden, grinding squeal of the air brakes and the lurching deceleration pulled him out of a deep sleep. 

He blinked his eyes open in the dark compartment. Above him, he could hear the soft, rhythmic breathing of Luna and Stella in the upper bunks. 

The train ground to a complete halt, hissing loudly. 

Leo carefully slipped out from under Olivia's arm, making sure not to wake her. He sat up and pulled back the heavy fabric curtain covering the window. 

The platform outside was bathed in the harsh, yellow glare of sodium vapor streetlights. The sign hanging from the station canopy read *Salt Lake City*. Beyond the platform, the distant, glowing grid of the city was completely enveloped by the towering, pitch-black silhouettes of the Wasatch Mountains. 

Salt Lake City sat at roughly 4,200 feet above sea level. It was significantly lower than the towering peak of the Moffat Tunnel they had traversed yesterday, so Stella had removed his pressurized oxygen mask before bed. But as Leo took a breath in the quiet dark of the cabin, he could still feel the lingering strain in his lungs. The air here was still thin, still dry, and his body was deeply, intrinsically exhausted by the lack of oxygen. 

He stared out the window at the glowing streetlights. Part of him desperately wanted to see the city, to understand what a metropolis built in the middle of an arid, mountainous desert looked like. He had read about the Great Salt Lake, and he knew they were about to cross the desolate salt flats in the dark. 

But as his chest heaved with a shallow, slightly labored breath, the other part of him knew the truth: he just wanted to go down. He wanted the thick, heavy, humid air of sea level. 

Olivia stirred beside him, blinking groggily in the dim light spilling through the window. "Are we there?" she whispered, her voice rough with sleep. 

Leo reached for his NovaVox resting on the edge of the mattress. He turned the volume dial down to its lowest setting so he wouldn't wake his sisters above them. 

<aac>Salt Lake City,</aac> the device whispered into the dark room. 

Olivia let out a soft groan and checked her watch. "Past midnight. Go back to sleep, Captain. When we wake up, the mountains will be gone."

She reached out and gently pulled him back down onto the mattress. Leo let the curtain fall shut, plunging the room back into complete darkness. He closed his eyes, listening to the heavy diesel engines rev up again. With a sharp metallic clang, the train pulled out of the station, leaving the mountains behind and pushing out into the vast, empty darkness of the Nevada desert.

### Part 2: The Nevada Desert

**Date:** 2003-06-30 at 07:30 America/Los_Angeles

When Leo woke up the next morning, the world had fundamentally changed. 

The heavy, oppressive weight on his chest was finally gone. He took a long, deep breath of the air circulating through the Family Bedroom, feeling his lungs expand fully for the first time in two days. The train had finally descended from the dizzying heights of the Rockies and the Utah plateau. They were breathing normal, oxygen-rich air again.

He shifted slightly on the bottom bunk. Olivia was already awake, sitting on the end of the mattress with her legs crossed, staring out the window with a cup of coffee she must have grabbed from the end of the hall. 

"Morning, Captain," she smiled softly, noticing him move. "Breathe easier?"

Leo nodded emphatically. He reached for his NovaVox, firing it up. <aac>Where are we?</aac>

"Nevada," Olivia said, pointing out the window. "We crossed the salt flats while you were sleeping."

Leo dragged himself over to the window and looked out. The landscape outside was completely different from the red rocks of yesterday. It was a vast, flat expanse of pale, sun-bleached desert scrub stretching endlessly toward distant, hazy mountain ranges. It was completely barren, yet beautiful in its absolute desolation. 

Above them, there was a loud thud and a groan as Stella swung her legs over the edge of the upper bunk, climbing down the small ladder. Luna followed a moment later, her hair an absolute mess. 

"Alright, fortress, rise and shine," Stella announced, stretching her arms and suppressing a yawn. "We have a very important mission to accomplish this morning."

<aac>What mission?</aac> Leo typed, his NovaVox speaking into the quiet room. 

"Breakfast," Stella grinned. "And the dining car operates on a first-come, first-served basis in the mornings. Which means if we don't get up there right now, we're going to be waiting in the hallway for a booth. Let's move."

Ten minutes later, the pack was making the trek back upstairs. Olivia smoothly hoisted Leo into a carry, navigating the swaying corridors with the same practiced ease as the day before. 

When they arrived in the dining car, the air was filled with the smell of sizzling bacon and fresh coffee. They managed to snag the last open booth by the window just as the train blasted its horn, tearing across the empty Nevada desert. 

The waiter handed them the breakfast menus. "Good morning, folks. What can I start you with?"

"I'll take a coffee with cream and sugar," Stella said smoothly, already knowing exactly what the rest of the pack needed. "An orange juice for her, and two chocolate milks for these two. For food, we'll take two orders of plain pancakes for the twins. No butter, no powdered sugar, no fruit compote on top. Just plain pancakes and a side of bacon."

The waiter jotted it down with a nod. "Two plain stacks, coming right up."

Leo looked across the table at his twin sister. Luna was already staring out the window, mesmerized by the hypnotic, blurring motion of the desert scrub. She caught his eye and smiled, tapping the edge of the table. 

They were back on level ground. They could breathe again. And today, they were finally going to reach California.

### Part 3: The Donner Pass

**Date:** 2003-06-30 at 13:30 America/Los_Angeles

By early afternoon, the vast, pale scrub brush of the Nevada desert finally began to give way to rising terrain. 

The *Western Pioneer* had left Reno behind and was now beginning its final, grueling climb of the journey: scaling the Sierra Nevada mountain range to cross into California.

Leo was sitting in his manual wheelchair in the Family Bedroom, having finally transferred out of the bunks for the afternoon. He had his NovaVox mounted securely on its metal swing-arm, typing idly as he watched the scenery change. The desert faded into thick, towering forests of incredibly tall pine trees. The train twisted and turned, carving its way up the side of the mountain range. 

"We're crossing the border right now," Stella announced, looking at the route map she had picked up in the Sightseer Lounge earlier. "Welcome to California, everyone."

"Finally," Olivia sighed, stretching her legs out across the lower bunk. "My back is starting to permanently mold to the shape of these Amtrak beds."

The train plunged into a long, dark snow shed—a massive, wooden tunnel built over the tracks to protect them from winter avalanches. When they burst back out into the sunlight, they were at the summit of Donner Pass. 

Leo stared out the window. Unlike the Rocky Mountains, which were jagged and terrifyingly steep, the Sierras felt immensely heavy and ancient. He could see Donner Lake sparkling like a sapphire far below them in the valley. 

They were over 7,000 feet high again, but this time, the altitude didn't bother him. His body had already run the grueling gauntlet of the Rockies; this climb felt completely manageable in comparison. 

<aac>How much longer?</aac> Leo typed, his NovaVox echoing in the cabin. 

"We've hit the peak," Stella said, tracing her finger down the map. "From here, we start the long descent into the Sacramento Valley. We'll hit Emeryville by late afternoon. Then we just have to hop on the Thruway bus across the Bay Bridge into San Francisco."

Leo looked over at Luna. She was sitting on the floor, leaning against his wheelchair, completely engrossed in watching the towering pine trees blur past the glass. She caught him looking and reached up, giving his hand a quick, excited squeeze. 

They had crossed the entire North American continent. They had survived the Midwest, the Rockies, the deserts, and the Sierras. 

In just a few short hours, they were going to see the ocean.

### Part 4: Arrival in the Bay

**Date:** 2003-06-30 at 17:00 America/Los_Angeles

The *Western Pioneer* finally hissed to a halt at the concrete platform of the Emeryville station late in the afternoon. 

The air outside the train felt fundamentally different. The dry, thin chill of the mountains and the desert was gone, replaced by a cool, heavy, salt-tinged breeze rolling in off the San Francisco Bay. 

"End of the line for the train, folks," the conductor announced as he lowered the metal bridge to the platform. 

The pack spilled out onto the concrete. After two and a half days confined to the narrow, swaying carriages of the train, the stationary ground beneath their feet felt almost strange. Olivia and Stella were both carrying two massive, heavy duffel bags, burdened with all the supplies they needed for a month-long stay on the West Coast. 

Leo pushed the handrims of his manual wheelchair, rolling easily down the long platform towards the station building. He looked around the bustling transit hub. He knew from his obsessive research before the trip that Emeryville was a major connection point for the East Bay. 

He stopped and tapped quickly on his NovaVox. 

<aac>Why aren't we taking BATS?</aac> the device asked, referencing the heavy-rail Bay Area Transit System that ran directly under the bay into the city. 

"Two reasons," Stella answered, adjusting the strap of a heavy duffel bag on her shoulder. "One: we have way too much luggage to be dragging it up and down the escalators in the BATS stations. And two: our train tickets actually go all the way into the city. ARTS operates a dedicated Thruway bus that connects right here."

She pointed toward the front of the station, where a massive, silver, wheelchair-accessible motorcoach was already idling at the curb. 

"The bus goes straight across the Bay Bridge," Stella explained as they walked toward it. "And it drops us off directly at the San Francisco Ferry Building. Our hotel is literally right across the Embarcadero. It's the flattest part of the entire city."

That sounded perfect to Leo. He expertly maneuvered his wheelchair onto the motorized lift of the coach bus, and a few minutes later, they were merging onto the highway. 

However, they immediately slammed into a wall of cars. It was five o'clock in the evening, and the legendary Bay Area rush hour was in full swing. 

"Well, we're not going anywhere fast," Olivia said, leaning her head against the bus window as they crept inch-by-inch through the massive concrete maze of the toll plaza. 

But Leo didn't mind the gridlock at all. Because they were heading westbound into San Francisco, their bus was routed onto the **upper deck** of the Bay Bridge. Sitting high up in the motorcoach on the top level of the bridge gave them a completely unobstructed, sweeping view of the choppy, dark blue water of the bay. In the distance, the iconic, fog-draped skyline of San Francisco rose up like a city of glass and steel, framed by the rolling green hills of the peninsula. 

It took them almost an hour to crawl across the bridge, but when the bus finally pulled up to the curb outside the historic Ferry Building, the sun was just beginning to dip low over the city. 

Leo rolled down the ramp and onto the wide, paved sidewalk of the Embarcadero. Stella was right. After navigating the uneven bricks, steep curbs, and narrow hallways of the East Coast for his entire life, the Embarcadero was a revelation. It was incredibly wide, perfectly paved, and completely flat. 

He didn't have to fight his wheelchair. He didn't have to scan the ground for obstacles. He could just roll. 

"Hotel is right there," Olivia pointed, hoisting her duffel bags again as she gestured to an elegant, towering building just across the wide boulevard with the glowing words *The Sovereign Regency San Francisco* affixed to the stone facade. 

Leo took a deep breath of the heavy, salty sea air. They were thousands of miles away from the intense humidity and quiet isolation of their small house in Virginia. They were on the edge of the Pacific Ocean. And for the next month, this was going to be their basecamp.

### Part 5: The Sovereign Regency San Francisco

**Date:** 2003-06-30 at 18:00 America/Los_Angeles

The crosswalk over the Embarcadero was incredibly wide, featuring long, steady pedestrian signals that gave Leo plenty of time to roll across the busy boulevard. 

The glowing letters of *The Sovereign Regency San Francisco* dominated the skyline directly in front of them, claiming the iconic waterfront property. 

They passed through the sliding glass doors into an absolutely massive, sprawling atrium. 

"Okay, this is officially the fanciest place we've ever stayed," Luna whispered, tilting her head back to look at the towering, multi-story open space inside the hotel. 

"I managed to get us a fantastic long-term rate," Stella said, looking incredibly relieved to finally drop the heavy duffel bags onto a polished luggage cart. "We're going to be here for the entire month of July. I wanted to make sure it was comfortable."

Ten minutes later, the pack was unlocking the door to a massive corner suite on the twelfth floor. 

Leo rolled into the room and immediately gravitated toward the floor-to-ceiling windows. The view was staggering. He was looking directly out over the Embarcadero. The Ferry Building's clock tower was brightly illuminated right below them, and stretching out into the dark water of the bay was the massive, glittering span of the Bay Bridge they had just crawled across. 

"Wow," Olivia breathed, dropping the last of the luggage on the floor and coming to stand beside Leo. "You definitely picked the right room, Stell."

"I try," Stella said, collapsing onto the edge of one of the massive, plush king-sized beds with a heavy sigh. "Alright. We've officially made it to the West Coast. We don't have anywhere to be tomorrow, no trains to catch, no schedules to follow."

Leo reached for his NovaVox. <aac>What's for dinner?</aac> he typed. 

Stella laughed, rubbing her tired eyes. "I am absolutely not leaving this room again tonight. But there is a massive menu of room service right there on the desk. You guys pick whatever you want, and I'll call it in. The hotel is paying."

Leo grinned, maneuvering his wheelchair over to the desk to grab the leather-bound room service menu. They had spent three days confined to a tiny, swaying train compartment, eating whatever the dining car had available. Tonight, sitting in a sprawling suite overlooking the Pacific Ocean with his pack, he felt completely, entirely free.

## The City by the Bay

### Part 1: The Historic F-Line

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 09:00 America/Los_Angeles

The next morning, the pack stepped out of the sprawling atrium of The Sovereign Regency and onto the bustling, sun-drenched sidewalk of the Embarcadero. 

It was their first official day on the West Coast, and the city of San Francisco was roaring to life around them. Just a few blocks away, near the edge of the Financial District, Leo caught sight of an iconic, wooden San Francisco Cable Car slowly ratcheting its way up an impossibly steep hill, its bell clanging loudly over the traffic. 

He immediately stopped his wheelchair, reaching eagerly for his NovaVox. 

<aac>I want to ride that,</aac> his device announced. 

Stella stopped walking, turning to look at the distant cable car. She offered Leo a gentle, sympathetic smile, kneeling down next to his chair. 

"I'm sorry, buddy," Stella said softly. "But those cars were built in the 1870s. They are incredibly narrow, you have to climb up onto the running boards to get in, and they have absolutely zero ramps or tie-downs. There is no physical way to get a wheelchair on board safely."

Leo slumped back into his seat, his excitement instantly deflating. The Cable Cars were the single most famous thing about San Francisco transit, and the fact that he was locked out of experiencing them because of his body was a bitter pill to swallow. 

"Hey," Olivia said, stepping in front of his wheelchair and pointing down the wide boulevard of the Embarcadero. "Don't pout yet. Look at that."

Rolling smoothly down the center of the street was a stunning, vintage streetcar painted in a vibrant, two-tone classic transit livery. It wasn't a cable car—it drew power from overhead wires—but it was undeniably historic and beautiful. 

"That's the SFMA F-Line," Stella explained, standing back up. "They run a massive fleet of fully restored vintage streetcars from all over the world right down the center of the Embarcadero. And unlike the cable cars, the city built elevated concrete boarding islands with wayside ramps at every single stop. They are one hundred percent wheelchair accessible."

Leo sat up a little straighter, watching the beautiful vintage trolley glide toward a station just a block away. 

"Come on," Stella grinned, pulling her jacket tight against the cool bay breeze. "I booked us tickets for a boat ride at Pier 33. The F-Line goes right to it."

Ten minutes later, the pack was rolling onto the vintage streetcar. The boarding island's ramp aligned perfectly with the trolley's middle doors, allowing Leo to drive straight on without a single bump. He parked in the designated tie-down area, watching through the massive windows as the waterfront blurred past. 

"Where does the boat take us?" Luna asked, her hands resting firmly on the push-handles of Leo's wheelchair. She walked and stood independently, but her Cerebral Palsy made her balance unsteady, so she frequently used the back of Leo's heavy manual wheelchair like a walker to keep herself grounded. 

"Alcatraz," Stella said, hanging onto a leather hand-strap as the streetcar rumbled along the tracks. 

Luna's eyes went wide. "Al Capone," she said out loud, her voice ringing with excitement. 

Olivia laughed. "Yep. The most famous mobster in American history. The government couldn't nail him to the wall for all the murders, the bootlegging, or the racketeering. They just couldn't prove it."

"So they got him for cheating Uncle Sam out of taxes," Luna finished with a massive grin.

"Exactly," Stella nodded. "They locked him in the inescapable island fortress right out there in the bay. And we're going to go stand right in front of his cell."

### Part 2: Ferry to The Rock

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 10:00 America/Los_Angeles

The ferry ride from Pier 33 was shockingly smooth. The gangway leading onto the massive boat was a wide, ridged metal ramp, making it incredibly easy for Leo to push his manual wheelchair directly onto the main lower deck. 

Once they were out on the open water of the San Francisco Bay, the biting, salt-laced wind whipped fiercely across the deck. The water was dark and choppy, crashing aggressively against the hull of the boat. 

Luna was standing right next to Leo, her hands gripping the push-handles of his wheelchair tightly to keep herself balanced against the rocking of the ferry. She was staring out at the rocky, isolated island looming in the distance. 
 
"United States versus Sullivan," Luna said, her voice loud over the roar of the boat's engines and the wind. "Nineteen twenty-seven... Supreme Court ruling."

Olivia leaned against the railing next to them, pulling her jacket tight. "What about it?"

"The Supreme Court ruled that all income, from whatever source derived, is taxable," Luna continued, stumbling slightly over the legal jargon but pushing through with sheer excitement. "They ruled that the government doesn't care if you make your money legally or illegally. You still have to pay... income tax on it. Even on stolen money."

"Wait, seriously?" Olivia laughed. "You're legally required to report stolen money on your taxes?"

"Yes. That was the silver bullet," Luna said, leaning heavily onto the back of Leo's chair. "The Feds couldn't prove Al Capone was a murderer. But because of the Sullivan ruling, they could prove he didn't pay taxes on his bootlegging money. It's the only reason he ended up on that island."

"That is the most aggressively bureaucratic way to take down a mob boss," Stella said, shaking her head in amusement as she held onto the railing. "Only Uncle Sam could take down the Chicago Outfit with an IRS audit."

Leo looked out at the island. As they got closer, the sheer, vertical rock cliffs of Alcatraz became intimidatingly clear. The massive concrete cellhouse sat at the very top of the island, looming like an inescapable fortress over the freezing, treacherous waters of the bay. 

He tapped his own NovaVox. <aac>How do I get up there?</aac> 

Stella pointed to the dock rapidly approaching the front of the ferry. "The Park Service has it completely covered. The hike from the dock to the cellhouse is the equivalent of climbing a thirteen-story building. But they run an accessibility tram called the SEAT. It's waiting right there at the dock, and it will drive us all the way up the hill to the prison doors."

"And once we're inside?" Olivia asked. 

"The main cellhouse is perfectly flat," Stella smiled, patting Leo on the shoulder. "You'll be able to roll right down the middle of Broadway and look straight into Capone's cell."

### Part 3: Inmate 85

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 11:00 America/Los_Angeles

The SEAT tram hummed quietly as it made the incredibly steep, winding climb up the rock face of the island, bypassing what would have been a grueling hike. It deposited the pack directly in front of the heavy, imposing steel doors of the Main Cellhouse. 

Inside, the temperature immediately dropped. The thick concrete walls absorbed the chill of the bay, and the air smelled faintly of salt and old iron. 

Stella grabbed four heavy yellow headsets from the Audio Tour desk. She placed one over Leo's ears, adjusting the volume dial on the side, before handing the rest out to the pack. 

"Alright," Stella said, looking around the massive, cavernous room. "The narrator says we start down Broadway."

Broadway was the wide, central corridor running between B Block and C Block. As promised, the floor was perfectly flat, poured concrete. Luna grabbed the push-handles of Leo's manual wheelchair, stabilizing her footing as she pushed him easily down the wide corridor. 

The scale of the prison was staggering. Three tiers of stacked, iron-barred cells rose up on either side of them, echoing with the faint shuffling of other tourists and the dripping of water. 

Leo listened to the deep, gritty voice of a former guard on the audio tour as Luna pushed him slowly down the row. Finally, they came to a stop in front of a completely unremarkable cell on the bottom tier of B Block. 

Luna pulled her headset down around her neck. "This is it," she whispered, her voice echoing slightly in the cold corridor. "Cell 181. Inmate eighty-five."

Leo stared through the heavy iron bars. The cell was terrifyingly small—measuring only five feet by nine feet. It contained a narrow steel cot folded down from the wall, a small sink, and an exposed toilet. That was it. There were no windows, just the concrete walls and the bars facing the corridor. 

"It's so small," Olivia murmured, standing next to Leo's wheelchair. "The man who practically owned the entire city of Chicago, who lived in penthouses and had police chiefs on his payroll, was locked in a concrete closet."

"No special treatment," Stella added quietly. "That was the entire point of this place. Once you stepped off that boat, you weren't a mob boss anymore. You were just a number."

Leo reached for his NovaVox, his fingers cold against the screen. 

<aac>It feels like a tomb,</aac> he typed, the synthesized voice cutting through the damp, heavy air of the cellhouse. 

"It basically was," Luna agreed, staring into the dark cell. "He spent four and a half years in this exact spot. And the worst part?"

She pointed toward the heavy doors at the end of the corridor. 

"On New Year's Eve," Luna said softly, "if the wind was blowing in the right direction, the guards said the inmates could hear the music and the laughter from the yacht club parties carrying across the water from the city. They could hear the freedom right outside, but they couldn't reach it."

### Part 4: Dummy Heads and Spoons

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 11:30 America/Los_Angeles

Luna pushed Leo's wheelchair further down the flat concrete corridor, following the directions of the gritty narrator in their yellow headsets. 

"Oh, this is the good one," Olivia said, pointing to the next cluster of cells as the audio track seamlessly transitioned. "The nineteen sixty-two escape."

They stopped in front of a row of cells. Unlike Capone's empty, sterile cell, these cells looked like they had been frozen in time in the middle of the night. Tucked under the woolen blankets on the steel cots were incredibly realistic-looking human heads. 

Luna pulled her headset down to her neck. "Papier-mâché," she said out loud, leaning over the handles of Leo's chair to get a closer look. "Frank Morris and the Anglin brothers built dummy heads out of soap, toilet paper, and concrete dust. They even swept up real human hair from the prison barbershop and glued it to the tops."

"That is wildly creepy, but you have to respect the craftsmanship," Stella muttered, staring at the dummy head resting on the pillow. 

"Look under the sink," Olivia pointed out. 

Leo leaned forward in his wheelchair. The small, rectangular ventilation grate under the sink had been completely removed, leaving a dark, jagged hole leading into the utility corridor behind the cells. 

"They chipped away the concrete around the vent using stolen spoons from the dining hall," Luna explained, her voice bouncing excitedly off the cell walls. "It took them months. They played the accordion during music hour to cover up the sound of the scraping. Then they climbed up the plumbing pipes, got onto the roof, and paddled into the bay on a raft made of fifty stolen raincoats."

<aac>Did they survive?</aac> Leo typed on his NovaVox, looking at the dark hole in the wall. 

"Nobody knows," Olivia said, pulling her headset off. "The official FBI report says they drowned in the bay. The currents are incredibly fast, and the water is freezing."

"They survived," Luna said instantly, with absolute, unwavering conviction. "They were smart enough to build a raft out of raincoats and dummy heads out of soap. You don't spend a year chipping through concrete with a spoon just to jump into the water without a plan."

Leo looked back at his sister, then turned to the window at the end of the cell block, which looked out over the dark, violently choppy, freezing water of the San Francisco Bay. 

He tapped his NovaVox screen. 

<aac>They drowned,</aac> his device announced flatly. 

"You have no imagination," Luna huffed, playfully shoving his shoulder. 

"I'm with Leo on this one," Stella said, shuddering as she looked out the window. "I wouldn't last five minutes in that water. Come on, let's go see D Block. The tour says there are still bullet holes in the walls from the Battle of Alcatraz."

### Part 5: West Coast Chowda

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 13:00 America/Los_Angeles

The ferry ride back to the mainland was significantly quieter than the ride out. The heavy, oppressive atmosphere of the Alcatraz cellhouse had left the entire pack feeling somber and exhausted. 

By the time they rolled off the gangway at Pier 33, it was one o'clock in the afternoon, and the waterfront was completely packed with tourists. 

Luna pushed Leo's wheelchair along the wide concrete sidewalks of the Embarcadero, following Stella and Olivia as they headed north toward Fisherman's Wharf. The air was thick with a chaotic mix of smells: sea salt, roasting nuts, frying seafood, and the overwhelming, yeasty scent of baking sourdough bread. 

"Okay, I am starving," Olivia announced, stopping in front of a massive waterfront bakery where dozens of tourists were standing in line. She pointed at a large picture on the menu board. "We are in San Francisco. We have to get the famous clam chowder in the sourdough bread bowls."

Stella took one look at the picture of the thick, creamy white soup spilling over the edges of a hollowed-out loaf of bread. Instantly, her thick, working-class Newport, Rhode Island accent—which usually only appeared when she was incredibly passionate or angry—flared to life. 

"That is absolutely not chowda," Stella said, crossing her arms defensively. "That is a bowl of heavy cream and potatoes pretending to be seafood. Real chowda has a clear broth."

"Amen to that!" a voice called out from the front of the line. 

An older man in a windbreaker stepped out of the queue, grinning as he pointed at Stella. He had an accent just as thick and unapologetic as hers. "You can keep your heavy cream! Tell me you're from the Ocean State."

"Newport," Stella smiled, her defensive posture dropping instantly at the sound of home. She gestured to the twins. "My brother and sister and I were born there."

"Tiverton," the man beamed, tapping his chest. "I've been trying to tell my wife for three days, you can't get real seafood out here on the West Coast! Anyway, have a great trip, Newport!"

"You too!" Stella called back, waving as the man rejoined his family and walked away. 

Luna leaned over the back of Leo's wheelchair, staring at the picture on the menu board. The thought of thick, lumpy, creamy soup making the inside of a bread bowl soggy sent a visible shudder down her spine. It was a texture and sensory nightmare. 

"It looks like paste," Luna said out loud, wrinkling her nose in disgust. 

Leo completely agreed. He hated soggy textures more than almost anything else. He reached for his NovaVox, tapping the screen quickly. 

<aac>Burgers?</aac> the device announced. 

"Thank you, Leo," Stella said, looking incredibly relieved as she turned away from the chowder stand. "Yes. Burgers. No soggy bread, no heavy cream. Let's go find a place that just serves a normal piece of meat on a dry bun."

Olivia sighed dramatically, mourning her lost tourist experience, but she dutifully fell into step beside Leo's wheelchair. "Alright, Rhode Island. Lead the way to the burgers."

### Part 6: The Crown Point Memorial

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 14:30 America/Los_Angeles

With their stomachs safely full of plain hamburgers on dry buns, the pack spent the early afternoon rolling inland from the waterfront. They navigated the grid of the city, eventually arriving at the intersection of Market Street and New Montgomery. 

The sprawling, beautifully landscaped public park sat right at 655 Market Street, directly across from the towering, historic facade of the Pacific Commerce Building. It was a beautiful space nestled into the Financial District, but the atmosphere was incredibly solemn. 

Leo rolled his manual chair along the smooth, paved paths toward a large, dark granite reflection pool in the center of the park. Two hundred names were carved into the stone rim of the water. 

Luna gripped the push-handles of his chair, stabilizing herself as she stared at the main bronze plaque. 

"December sixteenth, nineteen seventy-four," Luna read out loud, her voice quiet. "The Crown Point Disaster."

"This is the actual footprint," Stella said, looking up at the sky above the reflection pool. There was a massive, unnatural gap in the San Francisco skyline directly above the park. "It's hard to believe a hundred-and-thirty-five-story skyscraper used to be sitting right on this exact spot."

Leo reached for his NovaVox, his fingers lingering over the screen before typing. 

<aac>They were racing Sterling Plaza,</aac> the device announced softly. 

"They thought they were," Olivia corrected gently, reading a nearby informational placard. "Arthur Sterling was in absolutely no hurry to open his tower up in Meridian City. He only cared about safety. But the Crown Point developers decided they were in a West Coast arms race. If they had actually followed the specifications and installed the right wiring, Crown Point wouldn't have been ready to open until Spring of nineteen seventy-five. But they were so desperate to claim the title of the tallest building first that they squeezed their contractors to shave four months off the schedule."

"And it cost them everything," Luna said darkly. "They opened five days before Sterling Plaza. And that night, an electrical fire sparked on the eighty-first floor and burned the entire thing down."

Leo stared at the 200 names carved into the granite. He had seen the massive, blockbuster docudrama *Crown Point*—everyone had. But seeing the movie was entirely different from sitting in the physical, empty crater where it had actually happened. 

"Arthur Sterling ended up bankrolling the movie," Stella explained, wrapping her jacket tighter against the afternoon chill. "Every Hollywood studio rejected the script because it was too raw and too expensive. But Sterling wrote a fourteen-million-dollar blank check to get it made. He wanted it to be a global public service announcement."

<aac>Vanity over safety,</aac> Leo typed, summarizing the entire tragedy in three words. 

"Exactly," Stella agreed. "Sterling Plaza was built like a fortress. Crown Point was built for a headline. And because they couldn't just implode a burnt-out hundred-and-thirty-five-story husk in the middle of the city grid, they had to deconstruct it piece by piece over two years. Then the city turned the empty lot into this."

Luna ran her hand gently along the cold granite of the reflection pool. "It's so quiet here."

Leo nodded. Surrounded by the roaring traffic and towering glass of San Francisco, the Crown Point Memorial was a permanent, heavy reminder of what happened when people decided that speed was more important than building things the right way.

### Part 7: The Transbay Tube

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 16:30 America/Los_Angeles

The shadows of the Financial District were stretching long across Market Street by the time the pack finally rolled away from the Crown Point Memorial. 

"Alright," Stella said, stretching her arms over her head with a tired groan. "It has been a massive day. I think we need to head back to the Sovereign Regency, order some room service, and completely crash."

Leo immediately slammed his hand down onto his armrest, shaking his head rapidly. He pulled his NovaVox into his lap, his fingers flying across the screen. 

<aac>BATS,</aac> the device announced. <aac>Transbay Tube. East Bay. EBT bus.</aac>

Luna, who was leaning heavily on the push-handles of his chair, let out a long, exhausted sigh. "Leo, you have been sitting upright in this chair since nine o'clock this morning. You are running on pure adrenaline. If you don't go lay down, you are going to crash so hard you won't be able to move tomorrow."

Leo shook his head stubbornly. He pointed down Market Street toward the massive concrete entrance of the underground Montgomery Street station. The BATS heavy-rail trains were a major special interest of his, and the idea of riding one entirely underwater through the famous Transbay Tube to the East Bay was simply too intoxicating to pass up. 

"He wants a complex transit route," Olivia smiled, accurately reading his intense focus. "He wants to transfer."

<aac>Dinner in the East Bay,</aac> Leo typed, looking up at Stella with wide, pleading eyes behind his thick glasses. 

Stella rubbed her temples. She knew exactly what was going to happen. Leo was going to push himself past his physical limits out of sheer transit-induced excitement, and he was going to pay a heavy, painful price for it later tonight. But looking at the desperate excitement on his face, she couldn't bring herself to say no. 

"Fine," Stella relented. "We take BATS under the bay, we transfer to an EBT bus, and we find somewhere to eat in Oakland. But the second we are done, we are coming straight back."

Leo grinned triumphantly. 

Ten minutes later, the pack was underground. They fed their paper magnetic-stripe tickets into the automated turnstiles and took the elevator down to the BATS platform. When the heavy-rail train arrived, the doors slid open perfectly level with the platform, allowing Leo to roll straight on. 

As the train accelerated out of the city and plunged into the massive, dark concrete tunnel of the Transbay Tube resting at the bottom of the bay, the roar of the tracks echoed loudly in the cabin. Leo watched the dark tunnel walls blurring past the window, completely enthralled. 

By the time they emerged into the sunlight in the East Bay, transferred at MacArthur station, and boarded an EBT bus rumbling through the streets of Oakland, the adrenaline was finally starting to wear off. 

Leo sat in the tie-down area of the bus, listening to the pneumatic hiss of the brakes and the rattle of the windows. He had successfully orchestrated a three-agency, cross-bay transit route. It was a logistical masterpiece. 

But as the bus bounced over a pothole, a sharp, deep ache radiated up his spine. His eyelids felt impossibly heavy. Luna was right. He had pushed himself way too far, and the crash was coming fast.

### Part 8: East Bay Diner

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 17:30 America/Los_Angeles

The EBT bus pulled over to the curb in a relatively quiet, tree-lined commercial district in North Oakland, the pneumatic brakes hissing loudly as it kneeled to the pavement. 

Leo let out a heavy breath as he unclipped his chair from the tie-downs and rolled down the ramp onto the sidewalk. The late afternoon air in the East Bay was noticeably warmer than the freezing wind whipping across the San Francisco waterfront, but Leo was too exhausted to enjoy it. His spine felt like it was made of lead, and every bump on the sidewalk sent a dull ache radiating up his back. 

"Okay, emergency dinner protocol," Stella said, taking one look at Leo's pale, drooping face. She scanned the street rapidly. "We need something quiet, and we need a booth where you can get out of that chair and take the weight off your back."

"There," Olivia pointed down the block. 

It was a classic, completely unremarkable American diner. The neon sign in the window was buzzing faintly, but looking through the glass, the restaurant was mostly empty and quiet. 

Luna pushed Leo's chair the rest of the way down the block. As soon as they got inside, the hostess led them to a wide, vinyl booth in the back corner. 

"Help me up," Leo typed weakly on his NovaVox. 

Luna immediately locked the brakes on his manual chair. She grabbed him securely under the arms, and with a coordinated, practiced heave, she helped him slide out of the wheelchair and into the soft, padded vinyl of the booth. 

Leo instantly slumped over, resting his forehead against the cool, smooth surface of the diner table. The relief on his spine was immediate, but his energy reserves were completely empty. 

"I'll order," Luna said softly, sitting in the booth next to him. She looked up at the waitress. "Two grilled cheese sandwiches, plain, with french fries. And two large chocolate milks."

"I'll take a turkey club," Olivia said. 

"And I need the largest black coffee you have, please," Stella added with a sympathetic smile. 

As the waitress walked away, Stella reached across the table and gently rubbed Leo's shoulder. He didn't lift his head from the table, but he turned his face slightly to look at her through his thick glasses. 

"You orchestrated a brilliant transit ride today, buddy," Stella said softly. "BATS through the Transbay Tube and a flawless EBT bus connection. It was a masterpiece."

Leo managed a small, exhausted smile. 

"But you're paying for it now," Stella continued gently. "So here is the plan. We are eating dinner, I am calling a minivan cab to pick us up at the curb, and they are driving us straight across the Bay Bridge to the hotel. And tomorrow is a zero-impact day. No ferries, no mountains, no complex transfers. We are staying on flat ground."

<aac>Thank you,</aac> Leo tapped slowly on his NovaVox, closing his eyes as he waited for his grilled cheese.

### Part 9: The Bridge at Night

**Date:** 2003-07-01 at 18:30 America/Los_Angeles

By the time the pack finished their grilled cheese sandwiches and walked out of the diner, the sun was beginning to set over the East Bay. 

Leo sat heavily in his wheelchair on the sidewalk. He reached for his NovaVox, his movements sluggish. <aac>BATS,</aac> he typed weakly. 

"No," Stella said firmly, standing on the curb with her phone in her hand. "No more transit today. You have absolutely zero energy left to navigate a crowded train station, Leo. I just called for a minivan cab."

Leo let his hand drop from the tablet, too exhausted to put up a real fight. 

Ten minutes later, a yellow minivan pulled up to the curb. Luna and Olivia helped Leo transfer into the backseat of the van. With a quick, practiced motion, Stella pulled the release on Leo's manual wheelchair, folding it completely flat and tossing it easily into the trunk. 

As the minivan merged onto the highway and began the long drive across the Bay Bridge, the exhaustion completely overtook Leo. He had absolutely no muscle control left in his core. Sitting in the middle seat next to Olivia, he simply melted sideways, resting his heavy head against her shoulder and letting his body go entirely slack against her side. 

Olivia didn't say a word. She just wrapped her arm tightly around his shoulders, holding him securely against the motion of the car as they drove over the dark water of the bay, the glowing skyline of San Francisco looming ahead of them. 

When they finally arrived back at the Sovereign Regency, it took the combined effort of Luna and Olivia just to get Leo from the minivan into his unfolded wheelchair, and then up the elevators to their corner suite. 

"Alright, straight to the bathroom," Stella ordered as she unlocked the suite doors. "He needs to get the city grime off him before he crashes."

Because Stella had specifically requested an accessible room when she booked the trip, the corner suite was fully accessible. The massive bathroom featured a sprawling roll-in shower with a wide, built-in teak shower bench. 

Luna parked Leo's wheelchair next to the shower. Olivia stepped in, wrapping her arms securely around Leo's waist. With a coordinated heave, she lifted him out of his chair and gently lowered him onto the wet wooden bench. 

Leo slumped forward against the tiled wall, the warm water from the showerhead cascading over his aching back. The heat felt heavenly on his spine, instantly melting away the sharp pain from the bouncing EBT bus. 

"I've got the shampoo," Luna said softly, stepping into the shower fully dressed in her jeans and t-shirt, completely uncaring that she was getting soaked. She gently tilted Leo's head back and began washing his messy brown hair. 

Stella stepped in beside her with a washcloth, gently washing his arms and shoulders while he rested. It was an incredibly vulnerable, completely reliant moment, but Leo didn't feel embarrassed. His family operated like a well-oiled machine, taking over when his body failed him. 

By the time they got him dried off and dressed in sweatpants, Leo was practically falling asleep sitting up on the shower bench. 

The corner suite featured two large queen beds, normally intended to split the pack into pairs. But as Luna and Olivia transferred him onto the mattress of the first bed, Leo weakly grabbed Olivia's wrist. He didn't want to be separated. He was in pain, he was exhausted, and he just wanted all three of them right there. 

Understanding exactly what he needed, Olivia climbed into the bed on his left side. Stella sighed, too tired to argue with the logistics, and climbed into the bed on his right side. 

That left no room on the mattress. But Luna didn't care. 

Without missing a beat, Luna climbed into the bed and smashed herself directly on top of Leo, lying stomach-to-stomach with him. It was the exact deep-pressure strategy she normally used when he was having a severe panic attack, but tonight, it was simply the only physical way she was going to fit into the dogpile. The heavy, warm weight of his twin sister pressing down on him was instantly grounding. 

The second queen bed across the room remained completely empty and perfectly made. 

Surrounded and pinned down by his entire family, Leo was asleep before Stella even turned out the lights.

## The Sovereign Regency

### Part 1: The Atrium

**Date:** 2003-07-02 at 09:00 America/Los_Angeles

Leo woke up feeling surprisingly refreshed. 

The heavy, warm weight of Luna sleeping directly on top of him all night had completely grounded his nervous system, and the sheer physical exhaustion from the previous day's massive transit ride had forced his body into a deep, uninterrupted sleep. 

True to her word, Stella had promised a zero-impact day. There were no hills, no train transfers, and no rushing. The pack took their time getting dressed before taking the elevator down to the ground floor for a late breakfast. 

The lobby of the Sovereign Regency was physically staggering. It was a massive, seventeen-story brutalist atrium that looked more like the inside of a futuristic spaceship than a hotel. Concrete balconies stacked up endlessly toward a massive skylight, and illuminated glass elevators shot up and down the walls like rockets. 

Stella secured them a large table at the lobby restaurant, right near a glowing water feature. 

As Luna parked Leo's manual chair at the table and sat down, Leo noticed the group of four college students sitting at the booth directly next to them. 

The group was striking. Two of them were clearly identical twin sisters with fiery red hair and heavily freckled faces, though one was noticeably curvy and the other was incredibly skinny. Sitting across from them was a girl with brown hair and standard glasses, and a young man with messy brown hair and thick prescription glasses. 

The young man was sitting in a heavy, motorized wheelchair. Mounted on a swing-arm attached to his chair was a sleek, silver aluminum MagnaByte laptop. 

"I'm telling you, it's the perfect angle for the Ethics in Engineering paper," the skinny redhead was saying, pointing a fork at the young man's laptop. "We focus on Arthur Sterling's architectural choices versus the Crown Point developers."

Leo's ears perked up immediately at the mention of Crown Point. 

The young man in the motorized chair nodded emphatically. He didn't make a single sound. Instead, his fingers flew across the keyboard of his MagnaByte. 

<aac>Isabel is right,</aac> the laptop announced, its deep, synthesized voice cutting clearly through the ambient noise of the restaurant. <aac>The San Francisco developers used under-specced wiring because they were vain and desperate to win a race. Sterling didn't care about the race. He only cared about building a sanctuary.</aac>

"Which is exactly why he bankrolled the movie," the curvy redhead added, leaning over the table. "He wanted to show the world what happens when you prioritize vanity over safety."

"And the ultimate ironic flex?" Isabel grinned, gesturing dramatically up at the towering, seventeen-story brutalist ceiling of the hotel lobby they were currently sitting in. "Sterling rented out this exact atrium. Right here. They filmed all the lavish party scenes and the lobby scenes for the *Crown Point* movie in this room, just to visually capture the sheer, excessive vanity of the doomed tower."

Luna kicked Leo gently under the table, her eyes wide with excitement. They were sitting in the actual filming location of their favorite disaster movie, listening to college students debate the exact engineering ethics they had been discussing yesterday at the memorial crater. 

"Well, put it in the outline, Oliver," the brown-haired girl with glasses said, pulling a notebook out of her bag. "I'll start drafting the introduction. Eleanor, you pull up the real-world casualty reports from seventy-four."

Leo watched the four cousins—Isabel, Eleanor, Oliver, and Sophia—dive enthusiastically into their college project. He tapped his NovaVox happily. 

<aac>I like this hotel,</aac> his device announced.

### Part 2: AAC Solidarity

**Date:** 2003-07-02 at 09:15 America/Los_Angeles

As the college students wound down their intense debate about the ethics of the Crown Point timeline, the young man in the motorized wheelchair finally turned his head. 

Through his thick prescription glasses, his eyes locked directly onto Leo at the adjacent table. Oliver didn't look at Leo's face first. His eyes tracked down to the manual wheelchair, and then immediately settled on the NovaVox tablet resting in Leo's lap. 

Oliver's face broke into a gentle, knowing smile. He reached down to the joystick on his armrest and expertly pivoted his heavy, motorized chair, bridging the gap between the two tables. 

His fingers danced across the black keyboard of his MagnaByte. 

<aac>I saw you listening to us,</aac> Oliver's laptop announced in its deep, synthesized voice. <aac>Are you Crown Point enthusiasts?</aac>

Leo grinned. He pulled the NovaVox closer to his chest and typed back rapidly. 

<aac>We went to the memorial crater yesterday,</aac> Leo's device replied, its voice slightly higher-pitched than Oliver's. <aac>We were just talking about Arthur Sterling's timeline. Your paper outline is completely historically accurate.</aac>

At the sound of Leo's synthesized voice, Isabel, Eleanor, and Sophia all turned around in their booth, beaming warmly at the interaction. 

<aac>Thank you,</aac> Oliver's laptop said. <aac>It is nice to meet a fellow AAC user in the wild. I am Oliver. These are my cousins, Isabel and Eleanor, and my twin sister, Sophia.</aac>

<aac>I am Leo,</aac> Leo typed back, feeling a sudden, deep sense of camaraderie with this stranger.
